#2925. Federal Reserve Board releases results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 10:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

Key findings from the 2025 survey include:

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2917. Darius Acuff Jr.‘s frustration level reaches new high after Kings' 1-10 start

Darius Acuff Jr.'s frustration level reaches new high after Kings' 1-10 start originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

SACRAMENTO — The start of Darius Acuff's NBA career hasn't necessarily gone as he had hoped.

The Kings fell to 1-10 after losing to the Orlando Magic on Tuesday night and still search for their first home win of the season.

It took seven games for Acuff to get a taste of his first NBA victory when the Kings defeated the Dallas Mavericks over a week ago. The Kings had a chance to carry over the momentum as they returned to Sacramento to open a seven-game homestand at Golden 1 Center.

But they lost to the Los Angeles Lakers, San Antonio Spurs, Miami Heat and, most recently, the Magic, to begin this home stretch 0-4.

The Lakers and Spurs games were scheduled losses, but it seems like the Kings had every opportunity to walk away with wins against a struggling Heat team and a lowly Magic squad, or at the very least go 1-3 in that stretch.

Instead, the Kings are one of the three teams in the NBA already with 10 losses on the season.

"1-10 just don't sound right," Acuff said postgame.

Acuff, who is known for his even-keeled, cool, calm and collected persona, visibly was frustrated after Tuesday's loss.

So much so that he couldn't even find the words to explain what's gone wrong for the Kings so far this season. The 19-year-old was asked to identify the most frustrating part of these losses.

"When the clock says zero and we lost," he responded. "I don't understand. I don't know. I don't know what to do, man. I feel like we're trying. Everybody as a whole. We're just not coming out with the win. It's just, I don't know, man."

The emotions stem from Acuff's character and what he's used to. And that doesn't, and never has, included losing.

So this is foreign territory for a competitor like Acuff, who is making NBA history seemingly every night and joining the record books with basketball legends such as LeBron James and Michael Jordan, yet his only goal is to win games.

Eleven games into the season, all the losses already are taking a toll on the young point guard.

"A lot," he said when asked how much of a toll the 1-10 start is taking on him. "I'm not a loser, so it's not fun. But like I said, it's an 82-game season, so hopefully something will shake."

Acuff finished Tuesday's loss with 29 points on 10-of-21 shooting, with four rebounds, six assists, four turnovers and two steals in 40 minutes.

So far this season, Acuff is averaging 26.2 points, 3.8 rebounds and 7.3 assists in a league-high 37-plus minutes played per game.

It is early in the season, and the wins, although maybe not many, eventually will come for Acuff and the Kings. But after Tuesday's loss, the rookie seemed to be on the verge of tired of hearing that without anything changing.

"I mean, yeah, we've been saying that for five games now, so it's kind of hard to keep saying that without actually doing it," Acuff said. "We just got to go out there and actually do it. We're on the court playing. There's nobody playing but ourselves. Like I said, we just got to get better as a team. And to answer your question, it's just kind of the second half, it's kind of frustrating. It's like the first half we come out with energy, and then in the third quarter sometimes we don't, and then we do. And then the fourth quarter, the last five minutes of a game, we just can't put it together as a team.

"I think we're trying. We're talking as a staff, as a team. But like I said, when the clock hits zero, we just lose, and that's the most frustrating part."

The melancholy mood was the same when Zach LaVine approached the postgame podium. LaVine, who had five turnovers in Tuesday's loss, said he's "been in the league too damn long" to turn the ball over like he did.

But while LaVine also might have been disappointed with his team's start to the season, the 13-year veteran guard offered his own perspective on the current state of the struggling Kings.

"I mean, there's going to be outside people saying things. There's going to be a lot of finger-pointing," LaVine said. "I think, for us, we got to try to stay together from the front office down. We all have the same record right now, and it's us versus everybody else. So that's how we have to look at it. We have to try to figure out as a unit how are we going to, you know, start putting together more games where we can give ourselves a chance to win. We're trending in the right area.

"It feels a lot better out there. We've done a lot of better things, but we just got to clean up the small mistakes, myself included, to where we put together a full 48. I think then we get that satisfaction to where we win a game, and maybe we can start getting a couple more and more. The situation is what it is, but this is our job, and we have to figure out, at least as a vet myself, to keep guys uplifted, bring it each and every day, bring it in practice, regardless how you're feeling, I think that's just part of it. So I'm going to."

The irritation was apparent in the locker room, too, as most players already had left the arena by the time the media was let in.

De'Andre Hunter did speak in the locker room postgame, and his words immediately matched his demeanor when asked where the frustration level is at right now.

He also didn't sugarcoat his response when asked what the Kings need to clean up in order to turn a corner on the season.

"Everything," he said. "Just not good enough."

It isn't necessarily a surprise that the Kings aren't one of the top teams in the Western Conference, but this 1-10 start still feels worse than anyone could have imagined.

And judging by the players' mood postgame Tuesday, the frustration level has reached an all-time high.

There is the possibility that these built-up emotions turn into motivation that the Kings could use to start winning games. And at this point, that really is all they can lean on.

"That's all we have is motivation," Hunter said. "With losing, you can't really build any chemistry. No team camaraderie. But we just got motivation about the next game."

Download and follow The Deuce & Mo Podcast

#2914. Jets’ quiet trade deadline brings about big defensive questions

The Jets, a year removed from making splashy news on NFL trade deadline day, were quiet Tuesday.

They are 2-7, losers of their previous four games, and they stood pat.

Jets general manager Darren Mougey, who last year traded away former first-round draft picks, cornerback Sauce Gardner and defensive tackle Quinnen Williams, for valuable draft capital, made almost no moves this time around.

In the case of this year's trade deadline, the Jets were more defined by the moves they didn't make — specifically pertaining to fourth-year edge rusher Will McDonald IV, who requested a trade Monday, and cornerback Nahshon Wright.

There were reports earlier in the week that the Jets were offered a second-day draft pick (as high as a second-rounder) for McDonald, the team's 2023 first-round pick.

McDonald, who has 24 ½ career sacks, has seen his playing time dramatically diminish under head coach Aaron Glenn, who prefers more size at his position. It has appeared from the start that McDonald, who's 6-foot-4, 245 pounds, doesn't fit into Glenn's defensive scheme because he struggles against the run.

Will McDonald IV (9) and linebacker Kingsley Enagbare (52) celebrate a sack during the Jets' Week 8 loss to the Raiders on Nov. 1, 2026 at MetLife Stadium. Robert Sabo for NY Post

He was a starter in 2025, and the Jets picked up his fifth-year option, guaranteeing him $13.8 million in 2027. But the Jets switched to a 3-4 defense and added edge rushers David Bailey, Joseph Ossai and Kingsley Enagbare in the offseason, and that has turned McDonald into a situational player.

This season through nine games, he has three sacks, one forced fumble and 13 tackles in 263 defensive snaps.

Glenn said Monday he had a talk with McDonald last week and called it "a really good conversation."

That was before McDonald played only 23 defensive snaps Sunday in Kansas City, decided he's had enough and had his agent leak out his desire to be traded.

"He's a good player and I still believe that," Glenn said Monday. "We're going to continue to try to put him in a position to make plays for us."

If the Jets could have gotten a second-round pick for McDonald, it was surprising they didn't pull the trigger — unless Mougey is higher on him than Glenn, whose job is in serious jeopardy, and Mougey sees a better future for McDonald with a different coaching regime.

It's difficult to pinpoint what exactly went on there.

What's also difficult to comprehend is the Jets' seeming waste of Wright, whom they signed in the offseason to a one-year, $3.5 million contract after he led NFL cornerbacks with five interceptions in 2025 for the Bears.

It seems curious that a Jets defense that has recorded one interception in the 26 games Glenn has coached would not try to find a way to take some advantage of Wright's ball skills.

It was a surprise the Jets didn't trade him away considering that, nine games into the season, the 28-year-old Wright has played all of 22 snaps — all of which have come on special teams.

It came as little surprise that the Jets didn't deal away receiver Garrett Wilson, their best offensive player, or running back Breece Hall.

Those two players are considered the foundation of what Mougey is trying to build.

There, too, was late talk that the Vikings, who were desperate to land a competent quarterback with Kyler Murray and Carson Wentz not the answers, were inquiring about Jets starter Geno Smith.

But that, too, went nowhere, with the Jets retaining the veteran quarterback in his second stint with the franchise.

The only trade the Jets made came Monday when they sent safety Andre Cisco, whose playing time had diminished along with his performance, to Detroit for a sixth-round draft pick.

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:00 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 10 â 2 vote:

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

#2908. Injury pileup gets bigger: Brandon Williams out at least a week

Last night, Brandon Williams lasted just 99 seconds before sustaining an injury that kept him out the rest of the game. This afternoon, following an MRI, we've learned that it's a mild left hamstring strain that will keep him out for at least a week, which will be when he's next evaluated by the Golden State Warriors medical staff. It's a big blow for both the fifth-year vet and the team as this next 5-game stretch, already figuring to be the end of this historic winning streak at some point, could be a real setback.

As Brady mentioned last night, Williams joins Jimmy Butler III, Moses Moody, and Gary Payton II among the actively injured with Will Richard out indefinitely and Kristaps Porziņģis all but retired. That's 10 points, 2 rebounds, and 2 assists in 16 minutes a night that they'll have to make up in the near-term. It's not like solid backup point guards are easy to find, and the Dubs are already without a true center on top of all these injured forwards.

Still, it's worth wondering to what extent the team expected this. He had a bout of quad tightness a little over a week ago and was ruled out against the Suns; and as Anthony Slater noted earlier:

But left hamstring issues have followed Williams early in his NBA career. He strained it in the 2025 preseason while with Dallas and subsequently missed time last March while dealing with tightness.

The Warriors will need him back ASAP, but it's also early enough in the season where there's probably some incentive to let him take extra time to recover. All that to say that 7 days is probably the most optimistic recovery period. They're one of the trickiest injuries to deal with and it's probably easier to envision a far longer recovery period, especially since he had to come out of the game so suddenly.

So, the backup PG situation (non-Podziemski division) looks like this: De'Anthony Melton, Gui Santos, and LJ Cryer. Williams was a perfect change of pace. Like a shot of espresso off the bench. He provided such a jolt that that alone might be the hardest aspect of his game to reproduce in the aggregate.

#2906. Packers Film Room: Packers defense picks off Drake Maye 3 times

The Green Bay Packers stomped the New England Patriots this past weekend. Jordan Love was dealing, but so was the Packers defense. They picked off Drake Maye three times, and they started quickly, intercepting his first pass of the game.

The Patriots come out in 12 personnel (1 RB, 2 TE, 2 WR), starting in a completely balanced, 2×2 look. They motion a TE under the formation before the snap. They run a playfake away from the motion, then Drake Maye [10] starts to boots away from the fake, ultimately pulling up deep on a half-boot look.

The Packers are showing a single-high safety (Xavier McKinney [29]) before the snap, and that holds after the snap. Per SumerSports, the Packers rotate their safeties after the snap on 39.6% of their snaps, the 4th highest in the league. This time they stay in that single-high look (looks like Cover 3).

The Patriots are running Kyle Williams [18] on a deep post from the left, then bringing Mack Hollins [13] on a deep crosser. Initially, it looks like McKinney is going to be running with Williams on the post, but, as Maye climbs in the pocket, McKinney breaks down on Hollins on the crosser.

The Packers have the second level of their defense dropped extremely deep, which makes the throw to Hollins a difficult one. Evan Williams [33] is sitting right under the place Maye wants to go. Maye climbs up in pocket, and it looks like he's doing that as a way to try to pull those defenders away from the window.

While Hollins is looking to settle into a window behind Williams, Maye throws across the field, where McKinney is waiting.

McKinney returns it 36 yards, setting up the Packers with 1st & 10 on the Patriots 14. They scored 2 plays later on a Jordan Love scramble to make it 7-0.

The Patriots are in 11 personnel (1 RB, 1 TE, 3 WR) in a 3×1 formation. Kyle Williams is the lone receiver on the left. They've got a concept on the right that Maye can work, or he can take Williams on a vertical route if he likes that match-up.

The Packers are showing press coverage on the outside and their safeties are pulled up tight to the line. Evan Williams is the safety to the single receiver side. Just before the snap, he starts to bail to the middle. This looks like man-to-man coverage with the safety bailing away from the single WR side. That would put Kyle Williams man-to-man on Brandon Cisse [2] with no safety help, a match-up Maye clearly likes.

It's a really cool bit of deception from the Packers. Maye thinks he caught the Packers showing their hand a bit early, with Evan Williams bailing before the ball is snapped. Instead, it's the Packers showing Maye what he believes is a single-high rotation, but it quickly turns into a two-high look at the snap. Evan Williams goes from bailing to the middle to dropping wide as soon as the ball is snapped, with Javon Bullard [7] dropping to a deep zone on the other side of the field. Really subtle bit of deception here.

Maye has a bit of time so he ends up seeing that Evan Williams has dropped to a deep half-zone, but it's not a wide drop so Maye still thinks he can hit Kyle Williams.

As soon as Maye starts his throwing motion, Evan Williams looks like he's shot out of a cannon to the sideline. Cisse is in great position and he tips the ball to Evan Williams, who makes the pick.

It's impossible for me to see a tipped INT and not think of the classic Mike-McKenzine-to-Tyrone-Williams tip against the 49ers in the 2001 Wild Card Game.

Patriots are in 12 personnel in a shotgun, 3×1 formation. They are, once again, using Kyle Williams on a vertical route and bringing Eli Raridon [82] from the other side on a crossing route. To help spring that open, they've got Hunter Henry [85] on a curl route underneath. Push the defense vertical with Kyle Williams, pin down the second level with Henry and hit Raridon behind it. Nice idea.

Packers are starting with McKinney as the single-high safety, aligned extremely deep over the trips side. At the snap, he sinks down into a deep-half zone, with Evan Williams dropping into a deep-half zone on the other side. This looks like Cover 2, with Cisse passing off the vertical route from Kyle Williams to McKinney.

This should all work, but McKinney goes and makes a play. Maye hitches roughly a thousand times, looking to the route from Raridon in the middle. McKinney is still staying deep, but his hips are turned in and he's not even looking at the streaking Kyle Williams: he's looking to drive on Raridon.

As soon as Maye starts his throwing motion, McKinney triggers down, beating Raridon to the ball.

It was 17-7 at this point in the game. The Packers scored on a Kaleb Johnson rushing TD 3 plays later to take a 24-7 lead.

Albums listened to: Brother Bird – The Lodge in Miller County; Wilby – Center of Affection

#2901. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 stocks for the long haul

Global stock markets have been volatile as investors assess high bond yields and continued tensions between the U.S. and Iran. There are concerns about the impact of uncertainty in the Middle East on oil prices and consequently on inflation.

Investors who prefer to look beyond short-term noise and buy stocks with attractive long-term growth potential can follow the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts. The ratings of these experts are backed by in-depth research of a company's financials and thorough analysis of the factors affecting its business performance.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Semiconductor giant Nvidia (NVDA) reported stellar fiscal second-quarter results and reassured investors about continued strength in AI-driven demand for its chips. Moreover, Nvidia's FY28 revenue growth outlook of 70% was well above the Street's expectations.

Reacting to the Q2 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reiterated a buy rating on NVDA and increased his price target to $300 from $288. "This is our Top Pick in the semis group, with a compelling product cycle, exceptional growth and valuation below peers," said the 5-star analyst.

Nvidia delivered impressive quarterly results despite supply constraints, Moore said, but he believes that the FY28 revenue and gross margin commentary mattered more, with a favorable outlook for both measures.

Moore said Nvidia's 70% revenue growth guidance for FY28 was above his own estimate of 52% and the consensus of about 40%. The top-line growth estimate was a "remarkable figure" given supply issues and Moore said he expects Nvidia to continue to address bottlenecks to deliver rapid growth.

Among other takeaways, the analyst said performance metrics for Vera Rubin chips were strong, with Nvidia emphasizing a 30x higher throughput per megawatt and 35x lower token cost compared with Grace Blackwell Ultra.

Moore ranks No. 159 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 60% of the time, delivering an average return of 24.60%. See Nvidia Statistics on TipRanks.

Ride-hailing and delivery platform Uber Technologies (UBER) was recommended by BMO Capital analyst Brian Pitz, who discussed the company's opportunity in the autonomous vehicle (AV) market in a recent research note that reiterated a buy rating and price target of $119.

The 5-star analyst highlighted Uber's transition into a diversified marketplace, with investors now focusing on its AV growth prospects. The key question is how much value can Uber generate as AVs become commercialized, Pitz asked.

"We continue to believe that Uber's AV strategy is evolving and will become a significant driver of revenue and profitability," the analyst said.

Importantly, Pitz believes that Uber can establish itself as a preferred mobility platform for AV makers, supported by its growing AV infrastructure capabilities and an expanding partner base. This would enable Uber to win a significant share of value as the AV market is commercialized.

Additionally, Pitz noted Uber's enhanced position in the AV value chain, with the company shifting from serving as a distribution channel for robotaxis to becoming a broader platform for AV mobility. The analyst discussed Uber's efforts to capture growth opportunities in the AV market, including higher capital spending, expansion into new markets and building partnerships beyond Alphabet's Waymo.

Pitz ranks No. 574 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of about 12.20%. See Uber Technologies Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Semiconductor company Marvell Technology (MRVL) recently announced better-than-expected results for the second quarter of FY27. But the company's increased FY28 guidance fell short of investors' high expectations.

In reaction to the Q2 FY27 results, KeyBanc analyst John Vinh reaffirmed a buy rating on Marvell Technology, with a price target of $400. The analyst noted that Marvell's results and outlook were driven by strength in its data center business. The Q2 FY27 data center business grew 46% year-over-year, to $2.17 billion, far above KeyBanc's estimate of $2.09 billion.

Management "highlighted accelerating AI networking demand across scale-out, scale-across and scale-up architectures, supported by strong 800G optical DSP demand, rapid 1.6T ramp, broader 51.2T switch deployments and higher TIA/driver demand," Vinh said.

Consequently, Marvell raised its data center revenue guidance, now expecting 60% growth in FY27 and more than 60% in FY28.

Regarding the recently announced $120 billion warrant arrangement with Google, Vinh said management argued that programs tied to the warrant are already priced into the custom outlook through FY28. Meanwhile, new programs and those in execution are expected to drive significant upside to the previous FY29 custom XPU revenue estimate of over $10 billion, Vinh said, citing management.

#2897. Jordan Poole would like to come home

The Golden State Warriors had a poignant reunion on Monday night, when they welcomed the New Orleans Pelicans to town, and proceeded to crush them by 51 points. There are a lot of former Warriors around the league, but some feel different when they face the Warriors, and that was the case when Jordan Poole checked into the game.

It's no secret that it's been a rough few years for Poole, whom the Warriors took with the 28th pick of the 2019 NBA Draft out of Michigan. After showing some signs in his first two seasons, Poole burst onto the scene during the 2021-22 campaign, when he became a starter, played 30 minutes a night, averaged 18.5 points and 4.0 assists, led the NBA in free throw percentage, had a highlight every game, and was at times the second option on a Warriors team that would eventually defeat the Boston Celtics to win an NBA championship.

But things went sideways for there. The next year was bittersweet, as Poole parlayed his performance into a massive extension, but was also on the receiving end of a punch from teammate Draymond Green, which led to uncomfortable vibes and thick tension for the entire year. The next offseason, the Warriors traded Poole to the Washington Wizards for Chris Paul, and, away from the confines of Steph Curry and Steve Kerr's offense, Poole struggled, putting up big numbers but on very low efficiency.

He bounced back with a strong offensive showing in 2024-25, but prior to last season was traded to the Pelicans. His first year in the bayou did not go according to plan, as he averaged just 13.4 points and shot a lowly 37.2% from the field.

This year has been even worse. Poole started the season slow, coming off the bench in the first four games of the season and having lackluster numbers. That earned him a benching, and when New Orleans came to town on Monday, Poole had gone six straight games without getting on the court.

That playing drought ended on Monday. With the Pelicans down a handful of key players — including Zion Williamson, Trey Murphy III, and Yves Missi — and with the game a blowout from the start, Jamahl Mosley gave Poole his largest run of the season, playing him for 34 minutes. Poole didn't play well — he scored seven points on 1-for-5 shooting, with two assists and two turnovers — but it was still nice seeing him out there. And it was hard not to wonder: what could have been?

Maybe we'll get something of an answer someday. While Poole has been reluctant to discuss the situation with Green, Monday was the first time that he really spoke about his time in Golden State. And he didn't hide his feelings: he misses it.

Asked by reporters if he missed Golden State, Poole made his thoughts emphatically clear, saying, "Hell yeah! Obviously. They play the right way, bro. They play good basketball. They play team basketball … Do I miss it? Hell yeah."

Poole then spoke very openly about the difficulties of the current season, where he's struggling to find minutes on a team that is likely headed for the lottery. He didn't make excuses or cast blame anywhere, but repeated twice that the season — only a few weeks old — has been "pretty f*****g difficult."

His answer led to an obvious follow-up: would Poole, who will be an unrestricted free agent after the season, be amenable to a return to the Bay Area? Again, he left no doubt with his response, saying, "Yes. Yes. It just feels like home though, you know? Driving into the f*****g arena, crossing the Bay Bridge or wherever you're coming from, it definitely feels natural. And I think it's pretty evident, the way that I play is very fitting to how I used to play here. Nothing's changed, other than the systems that I'm in. We won a championship, you know, so it works … so to answer your question: definitely."

Poole elaborated, all but saying that he would jump at the opportunity to sign with the Dubs in free agency, should he be offered a contract. "I'm a free agent at the end of this season," the 27-year old explained. "If there's any place where I'm comfortable, it's probably where I started. So I just got to find ways to keep learning and try to make the best decisions for my future."

Barring something unexpected, the Warriors should have no problems finding the money to sign Poole next offseason, since his current play has tanked his value. A reunion would likely come down to whether or not the team felt they had a need for his services, and whether they believed he could bounce back following a few down years. He certainly seems like the ideal change-of-scenery candidate, and the Warriors are very familiar with his game and his work habits. They're always on the lookout for bench scorers, and especially could end up needing him if Brandon Williams departs next summer.

That's a long ways from now. But who knows: maybe this time next year, Poole will be back in a Warriors jersey. It sure sounds like he'd like that.

#2893. How much you can make in 2027 and still pay 0% capital gains tax

The IRS has released the capital gains tax brackets for 2027, which include a six-figure earnings limit for the 0% rate for married couples.

If you're eager to sell profitable brokerage account investments, higher income thresholds for the 0% rate could help reduce your tax bill, experts say.

"The 0% capital gains bracket is easily one of the most overlooked strategies for tax-free income," said Stephen Maggard, a certified financial planner with Abacus Planning Group in Columbia, South Carolina.

But it's important to know how the tax brackets work and the possible trade-offs before selling investments.

Despite recent stock market volatility, many investors have portfolio gains after years of market growth. As of market close Nov. 9, the S&P 500 was up about 10% year-to-date. The index also returned more than 16% in 2025, and over 23% in 2024.

If you're planning to leverage the 0% capital gains bracket in 2027 to sell or rebalance brokerage account assets, here are some key things to know.

The capital gains brackets — with rates of 0%, 15% or 20% — apply to assets owned for more than one year, known as long-term capital gains. By contrast, investments held for one year or less are short-term gains, subject to regular income tax rates.

Your rate depends on taxable income, which you calculate by subtracting the greater of the standard or itemized deductions from your adjusted gross income, or AGI. Taxable income also includes any gains from investments that year.

The standard deduction will rise to $16,600 for single taxpayers and $33,200 for married couples in 2027.

For example, let's say a married couple's AGI is $130,000 for 2027, including capital gains. After subtracting the $33,200 standard deduction, their taxable income would be $96,800, which falls below the 0% threshold.

However, taxable income should include mutual fund distributions, which can "dramatically alter year-end planning projections" and increase taxable income, according to CFP Jim Guarino, managing director at Baker Newman Noyes in Woburn, Massachusetts. He is also a certified public accountant.

If your federal taxable income is above the 0% threshold, you'll pay 15% on the portion of investment gains that exceed the 0% limit, up to that bracket's cap. Gains above that may be taxed at 20%.

Also, the 0% capital gains bracket applies to federal taxes, but your profits could still be subject to state-level taxes, experts say.

As income limits rise for the 0% capital gains bracket due to IRS inflation adjustments, more investors could have tax savings opportunities, experts say.

"There is tons of planning to be done here," especially for early retirees with lower income, according to CFP Dinon Hughes, a partner with Nvest Financial in Portsmouth, New Hampshire.

One strategy, known as "tax gain harvesting," is selling profitable brokerage account assets during lower-income years to leverage the 0% bracket, he said. Keep in mind, these sales stack on top of your other earnings, which could bump taxable income above the 0% threshold.

You can also sell the asset and immediately rebuy it to "reset the cost basis," or original purchase price, said CFP Charles Sachs, chief investment officer of Imperio Wealth Advisors in Coral Gables, Florida.

By resetting the cost basis to current market value, you can reduce future capital gains to trim your tax bill.

When selling assets at a loss, the "wash sale rule" blocks tax-loss harvesting when you rebuy a "substantially identical" asset within 30 days before or after the sale. But the same rule doesn't apply when harvesting gains.

Managing capital gains is only one part of yearly tax planning, according to Guarino with Baker Newman Noyes.

Before selling investments, you should know how boosting AGI could impact your broader financial plan, including income phase-outs for various tax breaks, he said.

For example, modified adjusted gross income can impact a range of tax benefits, such as Affordable Care Act health insurance premium subsidies, which can disappear once earnings exceed the federal limits by even a single dollar.

Another example is President Donald Trump's temporary $6,000 senior "bonus" deduction, which is worth up to $12,000 for married couples per year through 2028.

#2891. Traders bet on a pop for Anthropic's historic IPO

News of Anthropic's historic initial public offering provided a wave of relief for tech investors. The response in the perpetual futures market might provide even more relief.

As Anthropic prepares to raise money in its initial public offering this month, traders in offshore markets are making large bets that there will be a big pop on the AI giant's debut.

Trying to guess an initial first day IPO reaction is nearly impossible, but a new and fast-growing corner of the markets might offer some clues.

Pre-IPO perpetuals, contracts that aim to track the price of private companies, trade on decentralized crypto exchanges like Hyperliquid, Coinbase, Binance and OKX to name a few. The current Hyperliquid Anthropic contract implies a stock price of $98 and its market cap at roughly $1.7 trillion, more than 30% higher than the $70-$78 range announced in the company's updated S1.

While trading on the exchange is not currently available to American investors, about $250 million of volume traded across Hyperliquid's two Anthropic trackers in the past 24 hours, according to the exchange.Hyperliquid's pre-IPO Anthropic perp began trading two weeks ago at $138 before settling into a roughly ten-dollar range between $110 and $120 prior to last week's selloff in tech stocks, as the contract slipped below $94 at one point.To proponents of this new asset class, the pricing of the perps is evidence that bankers intentionally undersell equity ahead of IPOs to ensure a post-market "pop." Looking at SpaceX, the argument rings true: pre-IPO perps on SpaceX traded at a premium and converged with equity prices at the IPO, all before shares went on a three-day rally of as much as 40% in the immediate aftermath of the listing.News of Anthropic's upcoming roadshow may be weighing on competitor OpenAI. Perps tracking OpenAI's market cap dropped 7% to $1.27 trillion, according to Hyperliquid trading on a contract trading $2.8 billion in volume over the past 24 hours.Shares of Hyperliquid Strategies (PURR), the publicly-traded Treasury company investing in the HYPE tokens that power the exchange fell 4% Tuesday. The stock is up 150% on the year.

#2888. Here's the inflation breakdown for October 2026 — in one chart

Inflation throttled back in October from its blistering pace in September amid a more moderate rise in gasoline prices, but remained high as categories like food and housing put upward pressure on consumer prices.

The consumer price index, an inflation barometer, rose 0.4% on a monthly basis in October, after adjusting for seasonal factors, the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported Tuesday. That's down from 0.6% in September but higher than the 0.3% that economists expected.

"It's uncomfortably high," said Mark Zandi, chief economist at Moody's. "The Fed's not going to be happy about it. It certainly makes it more likely we get another rate increase in December."

The Federal Reserve uses inflation data to guide its interest rate policy. The U.S. central bank raised rates in September amid a recent pickup in inflation, which economists largely attribute to the war in Iran. It held rates steady at its last meeting in October.

Economists generally assess a measure known as "core" CPI — which strips out volatile food and energy prices — to examine underlying inflation dynamics in the U.S. economy.

That core metric rose 0.3% in October, as it also did in September and August. A consistent monthly reading around 0.2% would be more in line with the Fed's 2% annual inflation target, Zandi said.

Inflation has been above the Fed's target for more than five years.

The Bureau of Labor Statistics didn't report an annual inflation figure in the latest CPI report because of the lengthy federal government shutdown that happened a year ago. That shutdown, which ran from Oct. 1 to Nov. 12, disrupted the agency's typical data collection, and it didn't release an inflation report for October 2025.

"Investors do not need year-over-year figures to see that this was a hot inflation report and that prices are not on a sustainable path back toward the Fed's 2% target," Jason Pride, chief of investment strategy and research at Glenmede, a wealth management firm, wrote in an e-mail Tuesday.

"It would have taken exceptional evidence of disinflation to dissuade the Fed from raising rates in December, and this report delivered the opposite," he wrote.

Economists pointed to four big reasons for the hotter-than-expected CPI reading.

Perhaps the largest contributor to the outsized inflation reading in Tuesday's CPI report was housing, Zandi said.

Housing is the largest component of CPI, and movements in the category strongly influence the index's month-to-month reading, he said.

The "shelter" CPI index rose 0.3% in October, accounting for over 30% of the CPI increase, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics.

Zandi said he doesn't expect that dynamic to persist, however, because it's not consistent with the trajectory of market rents in the U.S.

"It does feel like we have a high level of monthly volatility" in the CPI data, he said. "It's all over the map month to month."

But economists said broader factors are putting upward pressure on prices, too.

"Core inflation today reflects three outsized shocks: tariffs, energy prices, and AI-related demand," Josh Hirt, senior U.S. economist at Vanguard Group, an asset manager, wrote in an e-mail Tuesday.

The Iran war has raised oil prices, pushing up prices for fuels like gasoline, diesel and jet fuel, which are refined from crude oil, economists said.

Gasoline prices rose 1% in October, a significant pullback after a 9.1% monthly increase in September, CPI data showed.

While diesel prices have retreated from recent record highs, they remain elevated and could spill more broadly into the U.S. economy in the coming months, economists said.

"Diesel prices have a really slow pass-through to all the areas of CPI, given it affects transportation costs," said Thomas Ryan, a senior North America economist at Capital Economics.

"These things take time," Ryan said. "It'll be an inflationary impetus for at least the next three to six months, I suspect."

There was some evidence of that broader percolation of diesel prices in October, such as in food prices, Zandi said. Higher diesel prices add to the cost of transporting food to the grocery store shelves, he said.

Food prices rose 0.5% in October after an unchanged reading in September, according to CPI data.

The run-up in jet fuel costs has also led airlines to raise their ticket prices, economists said. Airfares climbed 0.4% in October, after monthly increases of 1.2%, 2.7% and 3.8% in the preceding months, respectively, according to the CPI report.

Meanwhile, the inflationary impact of tariffs imposed by the Trump administration should fade by the end of the year, assuming the White House doesn't renew its push for more import taxes, Zandi said.

Certain categories of physical goods, like toys, furniture, and appliances, saw elevated inflation rates in October, likely because businesses have passed on tariff costs to consumers, he said.

The buildout of data centers that power artificial intelligence has driven up prices for electricity and consumer electronics, economists said.

Those data centers have led to a shortage of technology equipment, such as memory chips used in household electronic products like iPhones, Ryan said.

#2886. Swans CEO on transfers, finances and Snoop's next visit

Swansea City have turned heads by investing heavily in their playing squad in recent times.

According to Transfermarkt, Swansea had one of the Championship's highest net spends in the summer of 2025, while there was another significant outlay in this year's close-season window.

The transfer fees reflect the ambition of a club eyeing a return to the Premier League after eight years away.

But chief executive Tom Gorringe insists there is no danger of Swansea running into trouble regarding the Championship's new Squad Cost Ratio (SCR) regulations.

"We operate within the headroom of the financial rules," Gorringe says.

"Obviously in order to do that we have to continue to drive revenues, we'll have to continue to trade players at certain times and we have to keep pulling the various levers to make the best decisions we can to enable us to match our on-field ambitions.

"But we're doing that at the moment. We've got headroom within the previous regulations and the new ones. We are just looking forward looking to make the best decisions to drive us forward on the pitch."

Snoop Dogg sat alongside Tom Gorringe during Swansea's draw with Preston in February [Huw Evans Picture Agency]

Gorringe has been Swansea chief executive since March 2025, having been tasked with running day-to-day operations at the Welsh club by American owners Brett Cravatt and Jason Cohen.

He says Swansea's ownership group – which also includes the likes of Snoop Dogg and Luka Modric – are "really happy" with progress being made.

American rapper Snoop, Gorringe says, is planning another trip to Wales soon after his eye-catching appearance in February.

"We've got a few things few things in the pipeline there," Gorringe adds.

There is regular dialogue with Modric, meanwhile, over when his playing schedule will allow him a first chance to visit Swansea.

"We're moving forward on and off the pitch," Gorringe continues.

"I think we've built a really good squad, we're winning games, playing a decent style of football at the same time and ultimately competing with teams with much bigger budgets than we've got."

If a top-flight return is the ultimate target, Gorringe insists Swansea's approach is not purely motivated by a desire to rejoin English football's elite.

"It's not as binary as that," he suggests.

"There's lots of areas where we've set out targets and things we want to improve. There's lots of metrics for success and ultimately we want to be successful in all parts of the business."

Ethan Galbraith (left) left Swansea after just one season in the summer, but the club held on to Zan Vipotnik (right) [Getty Images]

In 2025, the headline deal in a busy summer transfer window was the signing of Celtic striker Adam Idah for £6m rising to £7m.

Significant sums were also paid for the likes of Marko Stamenic, Ethan Galbraith and Zeidane Inoussa, while Leo Walta arrived last January in an initial loan deal which became permanent for a reported fee of about £3m.

Swansea continued to spend during the close season, on the likes of Moussa Yeo, Joseph Opoku, Eli Just and Stephen Eustaquio, although the outgoings were offset by the sale of Galbraith to Stoke City for a reported £10m.

The Swans could also have sold Zan Vipotnik, but the only serious offer - £10m from Hull City - did not meet their asking price.

"The reality is in order for us to sell any player, the numbers have to make sense," Gorringe says.

"The numbers will make sense at a certain point for everybody within the squad. That threshold was met with Ethan and it wasn't with Vipi (Vipotnik).

"But we're really happy with the squad that we've built, with the balance it gives us and the options it gives us. Ultimately that's why we are where we are in the table."

Latest Swansea City news, analysis and fan views

Listen to the latest Swansea City interviews, news and discussion on BBC Sounds

Swansea are down to sixth in the second tier having lost four of their past five matches, but there is no panic at the club's Fairwood training base.

Despite their difficult run, Vitor Matos' team are within three points of top spot with almost a third of the campaign behind them, hence all concerned are fairly content.

The immediate goal is to re-establish momentum when Swansea return to action after the international break.

They will be straight back in at the deep end, with games against in-form West Brom, second-placed Middlesbrough and rivals Cardiff City to come inside eight days.

The January transfer window will mean a chance to tweak Matos' options, though Gorringe is not anticipating much business.

"The squad is full in terms of numbers - we're not here looking at a squad that's got lots of gaps," he says.

"Obviously if we assess it and we think there's opportunities to improve then we'll look to exploit those, but we're not sat here with a long wish list of things that we need to fix."

Filip Lissah has developed at Swansea after joining from Chelsea as a 16-year-old [Huw Evans Picture Agency]

The shape of the first-team squad is far from the only topic on Gorringe's agenda right now.

There are "really ambitious plans" for Swansea's women's team which he says will "come out into the public domain in due course".

At academy level, meanwhile, Swansea's attempts to regain category one status are ongoing.

Five years after the academy was downgraded to category two, Gorringe says a return to the highest tier would be "really important strategically".

"We want to be the hub of the best Welsh talent," he adds.

"We want to be providing the best possible pathways for children in this area to get to the first team, and we also want to attract the best talent we can from outside of that to benefit the football club. Moving to category one allows us to do those things."

Gorringe points to Filip Lissah, who has gone from academy prospect to first-team regular this season, as the type of success story Swansea wish to replicate.

"We want that pipeline to be continuous. We want there to be a pool of players coming through that can affect the first team and do the club proud," he adds.

Swansea must dig deep to keep chins up after Blues defeat

Swans boss Matos defends Stamenic over red card

While some focus is on stars of the future, attention is also being paid to Swansea's home.

The club are in the process of buying the Swansea.com Stadium, with Gorringe arguing it is important to "own your own real estate, have your own home and have full control over it".

"We've got plans to continue to develop and improve the stadium," he adds.

For the moment, that means the addition of new LED displays within the bowl and the building of a fan zone behind the South Stand.

But Gorringe also raises the possibility of expanding the stadium's 21,000-capacity at some point down the line.

"We have to be aspirational and ambitious," he says.

"Obviously we have to move one step at a time, but absolutely in the future we'd like to see that as an outcome, for sure."

#2884. Top Wall Street analysts recommend these 3 dividend stocks for higher returns

Dividend-paying stocks can enhance portfolio returns by providing a steady income stream to investors and the potential for capital appreciation. Several investors seek exposure to dividend stocks in a volatile backdrop to ensure stable income.

In this regard, recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, backed by strong financials and cash flows to support consistent dividend payments.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Energy Transfer (ET) is a diversified midstream energy company with about 140,000 miles of pipelines and associated energy infrastructure in 44 states. The company made a quarterly cash distribution of 34 cents per common unit for the second quarter of 2026. At an annualized payment of $1.36 per common unit, ET offers a yield of 6.3%.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Jeremy Tonet reiterated a buy rating on Energy Transfer stock and slightly raised his price target to $25 from $24. The analyst noted that the company delivered a strong Q2 beat across all key metrics.

Tonet highlighted that Energy Transfer raised its 2026 adjusted EBITDA guidance to $18.8 billion-$19.1 billion from $18.2 billion-$18.6 billion, reflecting robust base results and cost optimization. The 5-star analyst added that the company "tightened" its capex to $5.6 billion–$5.9 billion and expects capex above $5 billion annually through 2029, given solid opportunities.

About the Hugh Brinson pipeline, Tonet noted Phase 1 (1.5 bcfd) reaching full capacity by September 1 and Phase II capacity (0.7 bcfd) going online by Q1 2027. The analyst also noted completion of a 14-mile Hugh Brinson lateral in Abilene, Texas, and final stages of talks for an additional 250 mmcfd of Oklahoma power plant demand.

"All in, we see ET well positioned to continue capitalizing on organic growth opportunities across the value chain," said Tonet.

Tonet ranks No. 922 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 9%. See Energy Transfer Financials on TipRanks.

This week's second dividend pick is independent oil and natural gas company Permian Resources (PR). The company declared a base dividend of $0.16 per share for the third quarter, payable on September 30, 2026. At an annualized dividend of $0.64 per share, PR offers a yield of about 2.7%.

Recently, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Permian Resources stock and increased his price target to $27 from $22, citing an improved outlook on oil production growth and operational efficiencies.

The 5-star analyst noted that while PR stock has outperformed its peers so far in 2026, he sees further upside. Mehta is confident about the company's operational improvements and ability to deliver incremental free cash flow per share through the strategic ground game program. He added that the program has completed about $1.05 billion in bolt-on deals this year (as of August 5).

"We see the continued success of PR's ground game as constructive toward future inventory replenishment and increasing working interest over time," said Mehta.

The analyst also emphasized Permian Resources' solid track record of generating growth in free cash flow (FCF) per share. Mehta expects the company's free cash flow per share to grow at a 20% CAGR (compound annual growth rate) from 2025–2028.

Mehta ranks No. 401 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.8%. See Permian Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

Moving on to Sempra Energy (SRE), a utility holding company. Earlier this month, the company declared a quarterly dividend of $0.6575 per share, payable on October 15. At an annualized dividend of $2.63 per share, SRE stock offers a yield of about 3.1%.

Recently, Jefferies analyst Julien Dumoulin-Smith upgraded SRE stock to Buy from Hold but slightly lowered the price target to $97 from $101. The 5-star analyst noted that Sempra stock is trading at a price/earnings multiple that is 14% below its electric peers due to concerns about Texas transmission capital expenditure and the failure of California legislation.

Smith noted that the derating of weaker California peers PG&E (PCG) and Edison International (EIX) is weighing on the sum-of-the-parts (SOTP) valuation but is becoming less relevant given their materially higher fire risk compared to Sempra Energy.

Furthermore, Smith expects Sempra Energy's Texas capex plan to stay in place despite the opposition to transmission and data centers, though delays to 765-kV projects are likely. While a full recovery for SRE stock will need a clearer outcome from the Texas legislative session, Smith believes that the stock may already be an early opportunity for patient investors.

"We are early on the upgrade but see limited downside at current levels as the market is pricing in little transmission upside," said Smith.

Smith ranks No. 945 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 8.2%. See Sempra ETF Exposure on TipRanks.

#2883. Anthropic opens Australian retail IPO offering as AI scrutiny intensifies

Anthropic, the maker of AI chatbot Claude, opened its Australian retail initial public offering on Wednesday, giving individual investors access to shares ahead of the company's planned Nasdaq listing.

The offering opened at 10 a.m. Australian Eastern Daylight Time on Wednesday and is expected to close at 5 p.m. on Nov. 17, according to the timetable published by participating broker Stake.

Anthropic is seeking to raise as much as $64.4 billion in its IPO, with a separate $26 billion private placement, bringing a combined total to as much as $90.4 billion. Shares are expected to be priced between $70 to $78 each.

The Australian retail offering is expected to consist of newly issued Class A common shares under a prospectus to be lodged with the Australian Securities and Investments Commission, Anthropic said on its website.

Participating brokers include Commonwealth Bank of Australia's CommSec unit as lead broker, along with Bell Potter, LGT Wealth, Macquarie Group, Morgans, Morgan Stanley Wealth Management, Sharesies and Stake.

Anthropic said the prospectus would be available on its IPO website once the Australian retail offering opens. Investors will need to review the document and apply through participating brokers under the terms of the offer.

Australia is one of the small numbers of markets outside the U.S. where retail investors will be able to buy the company's shares directly, alongside Japan, Canada and parts of Europe.

The San Francisco-based AI startup has applied to list its Class A common stock on the Nasdaq Global Select Market under the ticker symbol "ANT." Anthropic said it does not plan to seek a listing on the Australian Securities Exchange.

The offering comes as Australia moves to tighten oversight of AI companies after an OpenAI agent gained unauthorized access to the medical statistics website of Medicare,  the country's universal health insurance program.

Prime Minister Anthony Albanese said Tuesday that the government would introduce mandatory reporting for certain AI-related incidents and establish an AI safety regulator with powers to enforce penalties.

"The law needs to get ahead of, and in some cases, make sure that it catches up with what is a very fast-changing world," Albanese told Reuters.

The government is also considering changes to criminal law for cases involving serious misconduct.

The country has also ruled out a copyright-law exemption that would allow AI companies to train models on Australian content without licensing it.

Open AI and Anthropic told Australian parliament last month that they would support mandatory reporting of data breaches involving their AI agents, acknowledging that disclosure is currently voluntary.

#2882. Apparently, kicking Devin Booker in the nuts is a judgment call

If there's one thing that gets my blood boiling and sends me rushing to my keyboard, furiously typing away while using this space as a personal therapy session, it's NBA officiating. And once again, we witnessed not only inconsistency during last night's matchup between the Phoenix Suns and San Antonio Spurs, but inconsistency in how the league ultimately assessed the consequences.

Let me set the stage for you. It's the third quarter, and the Suns are trailing by 20 points. Keldon Johnson grabs an offensive rebound and goes up for a shot from about three feet away. In the process, he kicks his leg out, making forceful contact with Devin Booker's junk. That's right. Right in the family jewels.

Booker immediately collapses to the floor, writhing in pain, and the officials? Play on. Nothing to see here. Apparently, a man getting kicked squarely in the equipment isn't enough to warrant a second look, let alone a review for a potential flagrant foul.

Now, when you watch the replay, you can make the argument that Johnson's leg movement was part of his natural shooting motion. Perhaps he was extending his leg to maintain balance while attempting the shot. I'll entertain that explanation because we witnessed something remarkably similar last week when Dillon Brooks attempted a jump shot with Jamal Murray defending him.

Did it appear that Brooks extended his leg a little farther than necessary? Absolutely. But anyone who watches Brooks regularly knows that he frequently kicks his leg out when shooting. It's part of how he stabilizes himself in the air. Perhaps it's also a technique designed to draw contact, but it's hardly an unusual movement for him.

Regardless, Brooks was assessed a Flagrant 2 foul and ejected from the game. Meanwhile, against a San Antonio Spurs franchise with a long and storied history of kicking the Suns in the nuts, Johnson wasn't even assessed a common foul. There's your first inconsistency.

Then, today, the NBA reviewed the incident and retroactively assessed Johnson a Flagrant 1 foul. So apparently, what wasn't even worthy of a whistle during the game was serious enough to warrant a flagrant penalty after the fact. Wonderful. Thanks for clearing that up.

We've been down this road before. The league acknowledges an officiating mistake after the game, but that acknowledgment does absolutely nothing for the team that was denied the call in real time. Granted, Phoenix was trailing by 20 points when the incident occurred. I'm certainly not suggesting that a whistle would have changed the outcome of the game.

But that's not the point. The point is consistency.

Johnson receives a retroactive Flagrant 1, while Brooks was assessed a Flagrant 2 and immediately ejected for a play that, when you compare the footage, appears remarkably similar. So what exactly separates the two incidents? Was Brooks being assessed a reputation tax? Is his history as a physical, occasionally antagonistic defender influencing how officials interpret his actions? Or are we witnessing another example of inconsistent officiating from referees whose judgment deserves scrutiny?

Because I'm beginning to wonder whether there's a bias at play when he officiates Suns games. Much like Tyler Ford, the official who ejected Brooks, Van Duyne has developed a history of calls involving Phoenix that have left me questioning his judgment. The officiating statistics surrounding both referees only add to that frustration, particularly when examining how frequently they've whistled the Suns for fouls.

And these two have history together. Remember when Devin Booker was ejected during a nationally televised matchup against the Los Angeles Lakers a few years ago? Ford and Van Duyne were both involved in the sequence that resulted in Booker receiving technical fouls and being tossed from the game. It's one of those moments that remains lodged in my basketball memory, and incidents like last night's do little to improve my confidence in the officiating.

This is the kind of stuff that really ticks my bombs. If the NBA wants to hold players accountable for excessive or dangerous contact, I'm all for it. Protect the players. Enforce the rules. Assess flagrant fouls when the situation warrants them. But for the love of God, establish some consistency in how those rules are applied.

While we're at it, how about a little accountability for the officials themselves? If the league can review a play the following day and determine that Johnson committed a Flagrant 1 foul, shouldn't there be some acknowledgment that the officiating crew, including Van Duyne, failed to recognize it when it actually mattered?

Because retroactively assigning a penalty might correct the league's paperwork, but it does absolutely nothing to correct what happened on the basketball court. And until there's greater consistency and accountability, I'll continue finding myself right here, furiously typing away, wondering how the same league can look at two remarkably similar plays and somehow arrive at three different conclusions.

#2880. NFL trade deadline winners and losers: Vikings go full dadcore with Kirk Cousins in Super Bowl quest

After what happened on Monday night, the Minnesota Vikings had to do this. In a dispiriting loss to the Buffalo Bills, the Vikings saw their tremendous defense go to waste, as Carson Wentz completed eight of 25 passes for 81 yards, no touchdowns, two interceptions, and a passer rating of 8.9. It was one of the worst quarterback performances in recent history, and it continued a run of recent QB futility for the Vikings, from JJ McCarthy to Kyler Murray to Wentz. Vikings defensive coordinator Brian Flores is out-scheming every other coach in the league right now with his laboratory of blitzes and coverages. But it's starting to matter less and less, because opposing offenses know that you can wait that defense out when there's no juice on the other side of the ball. The Vikings rank first in scoring defense (116 points allowed), but they're 27th in points scored (146).

This is not sustainable, which is why Minnesota welcomed ur-suburban dad Kirk Cousins back to the team he led from 2018-2023 by sending a 2027 third-round pick to the Las Vegas Raiders. The 38-year-old is in his 15th NFL season, and he's not quite the player he used to be, but he's a massive improvement over everyone else in the Vikings building at his position. This season, Cousins has completed 202 of 315 passes for 2,340 yards, 20 touchdowns, eight interceptions, and a passer rating of 97.1. The rest of the Vikings QBs this season have combined for 137 of 225 passes for 1,286 yards, eight touchdowns, five interceptions, and a passer rating of 82.0.

Cousins enters an NFC North that may be the most brutally competitive division in NFL history, but if he plays like he has this season so far, the Vikings have more than a puncher's chance.

Do the Vikings like that? We suspect that they do. And in addition to his play on the field, he should bring a maturity and calm to the quarterback position in Minnesota, something that McCarthy and Murray's critics have said they have lacked at points during their careers.

Related: Joe Burrow is reviving the Bengals and pulling away in the race for MVP

As for the Raiders, they essentially pick up a free third-rounder after signing Cousins to a one-year, $11.3m fully guaranteed contract, and selecting Indiana quarterback Fernando Mendoza with the first overall pick in the 2026 draft. The plan all along was for Cousins to start until Mendoza was ready for the rigors of the NFL. We're about to see if he is.

It usually takes a minute for a traded player, no matter how good he may be, to make a considerable difference with his new team. That player has to deal with a new city, teammates and schemes– it's not an easy adjustment. But when the Chicago Bears traded 2027 and 2028 first-round draft picks and a 2027 fourth-rounder to the Tennessee Titans for defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons and a 2028 fifth-round draft pick … well, it cost a lot, but it made Chicago's defense better – immediately. Per SumerSports, the Bears have gone from 6.39 to 4.45 yards per play allowed with Simmons on the field, and he's made other Chicago defenders better, from edge rusher (and former Mississippi State teammate) Montez Sweat to linebacker Devin Bush.

In Chicago's 27-24 Sunday night win over the Tampa Bay Buccaneers, it was that defense which took over, as the usually potent Bears offense had its issues against the Bucs' top-tier defense. Caleb Williams threw four interceptions, and Chicago won a game in which they committed five or more turnovers for the first time since they did so twice in the 2006 season – including a 24-23 win over the Arizona Cardinals that led to the greatest postgame press conference ever, courtesy of Cardinals head coach Dennis Green.

With the offense playing at its usual pace and the defense at an entirely new level, the Simmons trade is a prime example of what a big-time trade should do – turn a good team into a great one.

The Seattle Seahawks needed to revive a run game that has been below average since the team let last season's Super Bowl MVP, Kenneth Walker III, walk to the Kansas City Chiefs. They believed they can solve their rushing problem by sending a 2027 third-round draft pick and a 2028 fifth-round draft pick to the Indianapolis Colts for offensive guard Quenton Nelson, the eight-time Pro Bowler who will eventually find his way to the Hall of Fame.

Nelson still has plenty to offer at the age of 30. But these deals don't always work out the way teams hope. Seattle's first game with Nelson was a 34-17 loss to the Bears, who came into Lumen Field with Simmons in tow, and wrecked any offensive plans the Seahawks made. Nelson is a left guard, which meant that the Seahawks moved 2025 first-round pick Grey Zabel to the right side, where he hadn't played since 2023, when he was at North Dakota State.

Moving from one side to the other is never as easy for offensive linemen as people may think, and the Bears wisely lined Simmons up more on Zabel's side than Nelson's – 88% to 12%, per Pro Football Focus. This allowed Simmons and his energized cohorts to pressure Sam Darnold ceaselessly, and the quarterback is now out with a quadriceps injury and a cracked rib.

Backup Drew Lock started Seattle's win over the Arizona Cardinals last week, and Zabel moved back to the left side, because Nelson is now recovering from a knee procedure, and is week-to-week.

Why is this a problem? Because Nelson won't have time to acclimate to his new line, and Zabel won't have time to acclimate to his new right side. Plus, Nelson is in the final year of the four-year, $80m contract extension he signed in 2022. If Nelson misses significant time, and the 5-4 Seahawks can't find their way into the playoffs, whatever positive effects Nelson's acquisition might have provided will be meaningless in the long term.

It has become more than apparent that the Jacksonville Jaguars are all-in this season. Which makes sense. The 6-2 Jags lead the NFL in point differential (+108) and turnover differential (+9), and after their 33-10 win over the Baltimore Ravens last Thursday, they rank first overall in DVOA for the first time in the history of the franchise – which goes back to 1996.

Which is why general manager James Gladstone made the call to trade a 2027 second-round pick and a 2028 fifth-round pick to the Atlanta Falcons for safety Jessie Bates III. Jacksonville have been assembling their safety rotation with spit and baling wire of late and have a lot of injuries to deal with, and Bates is one of the NFL's better and more versatile players at his position(s). He's an expert defender everywhere from the box to the slot to the deep third.

The Jags also traded a sixth-round pick for New Orleans Saints interior defensive lineman Davon Godchaux. This also works because the 6ft 3in, 330lbs Godchaux is a good rotational run defender, and the Jags recently lost DaVon Hamilton, the heartbeat of their run defense, to a season-ending injury.

But as it turns out, those moves were just the opening acts. Gladstone then made a massive deal with the Philadelphia Eagles, trading a 2027 first-round pick and a 2029 second-round pick for defensive lineman Jordan Davis.

And this is where Gladstone and the Jaguars may be a bit over their skis.

Selected with the 13th overall pick in the 2022 draft out of Georgia, Davis was a highly-regarded interior disruptor based on his college work, but that promise never quite developed in the ways in which the Eagles hoped. The 6ft 6in, 336lbs Davis has all the athleticism and power you'd want, but the results have been middling to disappointing. Davis has never amassed more than five sacks and 28 pressures in a season. Those totals both came in 2025, after which the Eagles signed him to a three-year, $78m contract extension.

Davis has become an above-average run defender, but when you're giving up future first- and second-round picks for a player, you want more than that, right? Especially when you consider what the Bears paid for Simmons, and what they've gotten out of that deal already. Simmons is a truly transformative player; Davis at his level best is more of a well-turned cog in a machine.

Maybe Jaguars defensive coordinator Anthony Campanile can extract more from Davis than his Eagles counterpart, Vic Fangio, ever did. But given Fangio's overall expertise (more than half the defenses in the NFL apply at least some of Fangio's principles), it's a steep bet at that price.

– The San Francisco 49ers poked around the Colts' fire sale and plucked out DeForest Buckner for a fourth-round pick, and receiver Keenan Allen for a sixth-round pick. These weren't what you'd call franchise-transforming moves, but Kyle Shanahan's offense can really use some receiver help with all the injuries they've seen this season, and Buckner can add to a defensive line that has been similarly beat up. As always, it is impossible to write more than two sentences about the 49ers without mentioning injuries, and that dastardly substation curse.

– Speaking of Colts receivers, the Chiefs acquired Josh Downs and a seventh-round draft pick for fourth-round and fifth-round picks. Patrick Mahomes's team have been in receiver purgatory for a long time, and while Downs won't blow you away with his production (33 catches on 57 targets this season for 354 yards and a touchdown), he can be one of those sneaky-good guys with an understanding of the nuances of his position. The Chiefs' leading non-Travis Kelce receiver this season is Xavier Worthy, who has 278 receiving yards and a touchdown. Maybe Downs fills in the gaps. He certainly seems happy about his new home.

– The Bills made one of the day's smartest moves when they sent a second-round pick to the Titans for safety Amani Hooker and a fifth-round pick. Buffalo's safety situation has been less than optimal in 2026 – between CJ Gardner-Johnson (who was released after poor play and a spitting suspension), Cole Bishop, and Damar Hamlin, defensive coordinator Jim Leonhard has had issues. Hooker comes in with far more of a pedigree – this season, he's tied with Xavier McKinney of the Green Bay Packers for the NFL's most interceptions with five, and the veteran will help to knit the entire pass defense together.

#2879. U.S. gas prices fall below $4 for the first time since July

Americans may finally find some relief at the pump.

The national average price of regular gasoline fell below $4 a gallon for the first time since July, reaching $3.9788 on Tuesday, according to AAA. Prices are down about 17 cents from a week ago and nearly 39 cents from a month ago.

The decline comes after energy inflation slowed significantly in October. Energy prices increased 0.7% during the month, a far cry from the 5.2% surge seen in September, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. The BLS' gasoline index increased 1% over the month, after advancing 9.1% in September.

Bespoke Investment Group pointed out that gasoline prices also tend to decline at this time of the year, making part of the recent relief seasonal.

"While lower oil prices have been a tailwind for the relief at the pump, it's also perfectly normal for gas prices to be falling at this time of year, so if they weren't, there would really be something wrong," the firm said.

The drop at the pump followed a sharp retreat in crude oil. International oil benchmark Brent crude on Tuesday briefly fell below $90 a barrel for the first time since August as comments from U.S. President Donald Trump fueled hopes for an end to Washington's nearly nine-month conflict with Iran.

Recovering oil flows from the Persian Gulf have also eased supply concerns. Regional crude exports have returned to an estimated 80% to 85% of prewar levels, including pipeline shipments, according to ING.

"Energy markets remain under pressure as hopes for a de-escalation in the Middle East reduce geopolitical risk premiums. Oil extended its losses as Gulf supply flows continued to recover, while European gas also moved lower," analysts at ING wrote.

However, the Federal Reserve Governor Christopher Waller warned Tuesday that energy shocks may have more persistent effects on inflation than they did before the pandemic.

"While I am pleased to see energy prices down from their high rates over the past week or so, they are still quite elevated relative to the start of the year, and the ultimate effect on inflation will depend on what levels are sustained," he said.

#2878. Luka Doncic points to key area Lakers can vastly improve to bolster championship odds

The Lakers have the NBA's best record at 10-2. They've won six games in a row. They have the highest-rated offense (121.5). They're considered real championship contenders.

Luka Doncic #77 of the Los Angeles Lakers plays defense against the Atlanta Hawks during a game in Atlanta, Monday, Nov. 9, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

They're rated 19th defensively. Lakers superstar Luka Doncic believes they can claw their way up those rankings.

"I think we can be [good defensively], but honestly I think we just kind of relax a little bit a couple minutes in the game," Doncic said after the Lakers' 124-114 win over the Hawks on Monday. "So that allows the team [to] make the run. I think we just gotta figure that out. I think we can be way better than [19th]. I think we have the potential for that."

Doncic, who has been dragged through the mud throughout his career for his defense, has made marked improvements in that area this season, including finishing with 32 points, eight rebounds, 10 assists, five steals and two blocked shots against the Hawks.

Doncic, who leads the league in scoring at 39.3 points per game and is having an MVP-caliber start to the season, has shown much more effort on the less glamorous side of the court through his first 10 games.

Atlanta Hawks forward Onyeka Okongwu (17) vies for a rebound against Los Angeles Lakers center Walker Kessler (14) on Tuesday, Nov. 10, 2026, in Atlanta. AP Photo/Mike Stewart

He leads the NBA in defensive loose balls recovered (nine) and is tied for second in charges drawn (four).

As a team, the Lakers really falter defensively when Walker Kessler is off the court. And they're tied for second-to-last in the league in rebounding with 40.3 per game.

Atlanta Hawks guard Nickeil Alexander-Walker (7) shoots against Los Angeles Lakers forward Ziaire Williams (11) on Monday, Nov. 9, 2026, in Atlanta. AP Photo/Mike Stewart

But they're showing a lot of signs that they're putting in the effort to become a good defensive team.

Kessler, their defensive anchor, leads the league in contested shots (140), and Matisse Thybulle is second in deflections (50).

#2877. Aaron Rodgers is one win away from tying Drew Brees for fourth-most of all time

If the Steelers beat the Bengals on Monday night, Aaron Rodgers will move into a tie for fourth time on the NFL's all-time wins list.

Rodgers' teams have won 180 games he has started, counting the playoffs. His next win will give him No. 181, the same number Drew Brees retired with, and the fourth most of all time.

The only quarterbacks in NFL history who have won more starts are Tom Brady with a preposterous 286 wins, Peyton Manning with 200 and Brett Favre with 199. Rodgers has said this will be his last NFL season, so he won't catch Favre for third place.

Rodgers won 158 games as starting quarterback for the Packers, six with the Jets and has now won 16 with the Steelers.
