#2849. Landry Shamet’s simple approach giving Knicks chance to find stride

Landry Shamet has become Mike Brown's plug-and-play starter. If a wing player is injured or resting, Shamet is the man.

After years of accumulating spot starts as a reserve guard, Shamet said he learned how best to adjust to the bigger role:

Landry Shamet drives to the basket during the Knick's game against the Bucks on Nov. 10, 2026 at the Garden. NBAE via Getty Images

"I try to keep things as consistent as possible," he said . "In the past in my career, if it was a spot start or a stretch of them, I think I made the mistake of trying to change too much. Or think I had to change a lot. Or do something differently. Or be something different. Just because I'm starting the reality is it's the same basketball game. You can create different reasons as to why it's different. But for me, and I think the whole goal for anybody, is setting yourself up for success and for me that just looks like keeping things as standard and concise as possible."

Shamet started for the third straight game in the Knicks' 117-85 rout over the Bucks in which he scored 10 points in 28 minutes. He replaced Josh Hart, who was ill Tuesday after missing the previous two games with ankle soreness.

If Hart hadn't been sick, he would've played against the Bucks, according to Mike Brown. His ankle was better. Either way, Brown feels confident in the replacement and agrees with Shamet's assessment.

"With the way our team is built we don't need anybody to do anything extraordinary. Just go, like Landry said, be yourself, play hard," Brown said. "It's not necessarily always how you start games. It's how you finish. Landry is phenomenal to have as a starter because he's a two-way player. He has to be guarded and he plays with pace, a lot of other things that we believe in."

Landry Shamet handles the ball during the Knicks' win over the Cavaliers. NBAE via Getty Images

Shamet entered Tuesday averaging 9.3 points on 43 percent from the field — including 42 percent on treys. His production has made up for a rough start from Mikal Bridges, who was at 33 percent from the field ahead of the Bucks game.

Shamet was also closing over Bridges. He had 52 fourth-quarter minutes over the opening nine games, while Bridges had just 41. The Knicks were outscored by 33 points when Bridges was on the floor in fourth quarters.

Still, Brown most appreciates Shamet's defense. Based on the team's tracking data, the coach said Shamet has been the team's best front-end pick-and-roll defender this season.

Not coincidentally, the Knicks defense took off last season when Shamet returned from his shoulder injury in January.

To that point, Brown experimented with different starters when injuries cropped up. Miles McBride started 15 games. Mohamed Diawara started seven.

"Like we've always said with this team, that it's kind of a special attribute of our group that everything can change from night to night, from person to person," Shamet said. "There might be something required of me differently, tonight versus tomorrow night, might be taking more shots, might be guarding this guy. It might be being a great teammate on the bench and picking up somebody who's struggling or whatever the case may be. All these roles are constantly evolving and rotating. And it's all of our jobs collectively to just fill the voids that need filling when needed."

#2848. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

#2845. Investors pivot to defensive stocks as markets enter a ‘state of alert’

Markets are in a "state of alert," according to analysts monitoring a pivot from riskier tech sector bets into defensive stocks.

Technology stocks — a key driver of rallies in recent years — have been notably volatile in 2026. Concerns about artificial intelligence — particularly when it comes to capital expenditure, cash flow, debt and circular financing — have sparked multiple bouts of volatility this year.

The Democrats' sweep of Congress in the U.S. midterm elections amplified pressure on the sector last week.

Wall Street's tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index is up by around 8.7% since the start of the year as of Nov. 9 close, but gave up some of its year-to-date gains in October when it shed 5.3% over the course of the month. The index — home to Big Tech names including Nvidia, Microsoft, Alphabet and Apple — has staged a recovery in recent days, but remains in the red for November and is down 0.7% for the month so far after dipping into correction territory last week.

The Nasdaq isn't the only tech-focused index to have seen big losses over the past month. South Korea's Kospi index, which is heavily exposed to the semiconductor sector, lost 16.2% in October and is down more than 8% month-to-date.

The MSCI World Information Technology index lost 7% in October, and has shed a further 4.6% so far this month. MSCI's U.S.-only IT index is down 11.5% since October.

At the same time, stocks in defensive sectors have seen broad gains.

MSCI's indexes encompassing the consumer staples, healthcare and utilities sectors — widely seen as more stable assets in the equity market during times of volatility or uncertainty — have all risen significantly over the last month.

In a note on Monday, Alastair Pinder, HSBC's head emerging markets and global equity strategist, said recent moves in stock markets had been "vicious, and in our view, overdone."

"The market is already pricing greater execution risk and capex delays," he said, referring to the development of artificial intelligence. "With US semiconductors trading on a 12-month forward PE of 13.5x after a 45% de-rating since June, how much more pain is there to price?"

He added that fundamentals broadly appear healthy.

"The rotation into defensives looks extreme," he said. "Walmart trades on 40x forward earnings, near a record high, while Nvidia trades on 13x, near a record low."

Russ Mould, investment director at AJ Bell, described equity markets as being "in a state of alert, judging by how investors are rotating their sector exposures."

"Such movement matters, and can help stock pickers with their selections, bearing in mind the old adage that 'a bad stock in a good sector will outperform a good stock in a bad sector,'" he said in a note on Monday.

AJ Bell's analysis of 11 so-called "super-sectors" in the S&P Global 1200 shows that, in the second half of 2026 so far, healthcare and consumer staples stocks have been the best performers after energy — a major beneficiary of surging oil prices in the wake of the U.S.-Iran war.

The IT sector, the data shows, has gone from being the best-performing sector in the first half of the year on a 28.4% rise, to the worst performer in the second half after tumbling more than 11%.

"Information Technology has gone from penthouse to outhouse and slumped from being the best performer to the worst," Mould said.

"Energy has been resilient as have Consumer Staples and Healthcare, while Industrials have been noticeably weak. These trends could reflect nagging worries over the economic outlook for next year, as well as worries over whether air is leaking out of an AI 'bubble.'"

#2843. White House vows to press ahead with tariffs despite midterm backlash over rising prices

One week after voters punished Republicans over rising prices in the midterm elections, President Donald Trump's White House signaled it would stay the course on tariffs, even as his former top economic advisor warned that Americans are footing the bill.

Jim Goyer, Trump's director of White House Office of Public Liaison, defended tariffs as a tool to rebuild U.S. manufacturing, secure supply chains and protect national security during an address Tuesday at the B20 summit, a gathering of global business leaders in Washington hosted by the U.S. Chamber of Commerce.

"And we are just getting started," Goyer said. "Our ambition is to continue this pace for the remainder of the president's term in office."

But Gary Cohn, who served as Trump's chief economic advisor during his first administration, offered a sharply different assessment during an onstage interview later Tuesday.

"Tariffs are a consumption tax on the U.S. consumer," Cohn said, arguing that the duties are raising prices and forcing Americans to reconsider purchases.

The divide exposed a growing dilemma for Trump. His trade agenda is reshaping global commerce – and driving up costs for voters. Companies are adapting to the chaos, but the old trading system isn't coming back.

"President Trump has disrupted that system in a way that we're not likely to return to," Kevin Brady, a former Republican congressman who helped lead the B20 summit, said during a discussion with U.S. Chamber CEO Suzanne Clark.

The political backlash was evident in the Nov. 3 election. Roughly two-thirds of voters said Trump's policies contributed to inflation, according to the CNBC Voter Poll. Nearly nine in 10 said the cost of living was a major or crucial factor in their vote.

A separate Reuters/Ipsos poll released Tuesday found 62% of Americans supported repealing Trump's tariffs, while just 21% approved of his handling of the cost of living.

"Last week, American voters sent a clear message about the cost of living and their economic security," Clark said in her keynote address.

The pressure intensified Tuesday as new government data showed consumer prices rose 0.4% in October, exceeding economists' expectations. Annual inflation reached 3.7% in September, the latest available year-over-year figure and well above the Federal Reserve's 2% target.

Tariffs are not the only factor driving higher prices. The war with Iran has pushed energy costs sharply higher, while housing, healthcare and other household expenses continue to strain consumers.

But research has found Trump's import duties have added to those pressures. A Federal Reserve Bank of New York study estimated that about 26% of tariff increases were passed through to consumer prices, with some effects taking nine to 12 months to work through supply chains.

Cohn, now vice chairman of IBM, argued that while tariffs generate revenue for the U.S., those gains come at consumers' expense.

"You're looking at one side of the equation. You're looking at the credit side of the balance sheet," Cohn said, describing how he would respond to Trump's defense of tariff revenue. "There's the debit side of the balance sheet, which is every hardworking American who happened to pay the tariff through some product that they bought."

Still, Cohn saw room for a bipartisan trade deal. He suggested Trump and the incoming Democratic Congress could make protections against China and unfair trade practices permanent while rolling back tariffs on Canada, leaving less for future presidents to undo.

For businesses, the shift is already underway. UPS CEO Carol Tomé said customers are moving production and sourcing closer to home as global supply chains become more regional.

"Supply chains are diversifying. Supply chains are regionalizing," Tomé said during a panel discussion moderated by CNBC's Morgan Brennan.

Separately, UPS is opening a new $80 million-plus air hub in the Philippines in October to expand its Asian network and improve connections across more than 1,100 global trade lanes.

Other executives, like Jim Fitterling, executive chairman of chemicals giant Dow, defended Trump's tariffs despite their costs. Fitterling acknowledged they can drive up prices but argued they are necessary to combat unfair competition and protect U.S. manufacturing.

Democrats plan to challenge Trump's tariffs when they take control of Congress in January, but his veto power and broad trade authority could make them difficult to overturn.

#2840. Rep. Jim Jordan, Sen. John Thune elected GOP leaders in Congress

Republican lawmakers on Tuesday picked Rep. Jim Jordan of Ohio and Sen. John Thune of South Dakota to serve as their respective House and Senate minority leaders in the next Congress.

Jordan, the House Judiciary Committee chairman, was elected in a 106-73 vote, sources told MS NOW on condition of anonymity to discuss the private meeting. He beat Rep. Steve Scalise, R-La., the current House majority leader.

A two-decade House veteran and committed supporter of President Donald Trump, Jordan will take on the leadership role just as Republicans relinquish power, following blowout Democratic victories in last week's midterm election.

House Speaker Mike Johnson, R-La., announced the day after Election Day that he would not run for minority leader in the 120th Congress.

Thune, in a statement, said he "appreciated the opportunity to have an open, honest, and transparent conversation with my colleagues today, and I am honored to have their continued trust and support to lead the Senate Republican Conference in the 120th Congress."

"I'm proud of what this Republican team has accomplished together, and we remain united in our commitment to advancing conservative priorities, working with President Trump and our colleagues in the House, and holding the line against Democrats' efforts to enact their far-left, liberal agenda," Thune said in the statement.

"We have important work ahead of us, and I look forward to serving alongside my colleagues on behalf of the American people," he said.

#2839. Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double

Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stayed unbeaten Saturday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, earning a 127-116 win over the Bucks and improving to 3-0 on the season.

Joel Embiid had his 10th career regular-season triple-double, tallying 28 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists.

On his 30th birthday, Jaylen Brown posted 27 points and five assists. Tyrese Maxey had 26 points. VJ Edgecombe scored 17 and grabbed 13 boards.

The Sixers' absences on the first night of a back-to-back included LeBron James (low back/sciatica management). Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said pregame that he did not know yet who would be available for his team's matchup with the Pistons on Sunday.

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory Saturday:

Dean Wade made his Sixers debut and started in James' place. He'd missed the first two games of the regular season with a left ankle sprain.

The 3-and-D forward scored the night's first points by a sinking a corner three-pointer on the opening possession.

Embiid also had a sharp start. He moved well, made good decisions and served as the main hub of his team's offense in the early going.

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center has often enjoyed facing Myles Turner. He entered the night with an average of 29.4 points in 18 previous games against the Bucks big man.

Embiid did a little bit of everything in the first quarter, posting eight points, four assists and four rebounds over nine minutes. After snatching an offensive board, Embiid drained a fadeaway jumper to give the Sixers a 10-1 lead.

One of Embiid's assists was quite stylish. He found Edgecombe open beyond the arc with a behind-the-back bounce pass. Edgecombe knocked down the shot and wound up going 3 for 4 from three-point range in the first quarter.

The Sixers added rookie Labaron Philon Jr. to their rotation. Jabari Walker stayed in the mix, though he didn't enter until the 7:36 mark of the second quarter.

Philon played four minutes in his NBA debut and missed his lone shot attempt, a corner three. On defense, Philon allowed a couple of blow-bys off the dribble. He generally looked like he belonged on offense, albeit without any production in his brief run.

Maxey banked in a half-court heave at the first-quarter buzzer to put the Sixers up nine points. The Sixers couldn't carry forward any momentum, however, and began the second quarter poorly.

Adem Bona had another rough stint amid a subpar start to the season. After Anfernee Simons missed a transition layup try, Ryan Rollins' three lifted the Bucks into a 40-37 lead.

The dreadful stretch for the Sixers on both sides of the ball called to mind how they'd fallen into a 29-point deficit Thursday night vs. the Cavaliers before clawing out a comeback win.

The Sixers mustered just seven points in first six minutes of the second quarter and had many stagnant possessions that concluded with contested jumpers. The team's transition defense was dismal for much of the second period. Milwaukee had the game's first 19 fast-break points.

The Sixers regained the lead late in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope made two three-balls in the second and nabbed three first-half steals. Embiid assisted a go-ahead, and-one Walker layup.

Brown hit a three with 11.8 seconds left in the second quarter. Unsurprisingly, his variety of shotmaking skills were useful without James in the lineup.

Even when James or Embiid sit, the Sixers should have a simple advantage in terms of volume of stars over most opponents this year. None of Brown, Embiid or Maxey had the sort of off night that would have improved the Bucks' odds of a road win. They'd all passed the 20-point mark by the end of the third quarter.

Maxey notched another final-possession bucket, this time with a more conventional make. He canned a jumper from the right elbow with 1.7 seconds left in the third to extend the Sixers' lead to 97-87.

The Bucks made a late push in the fourth quarter. Kel'el Ware's put-back, and-one layup cut the Milwaukee's deficit to 117-114.

The Sixers ultimately handled business. Embiid reached his triple-double by assisting a second-chance Brown jumper and and Caldwell-Pope soon drilled a dagger corner three. Embiid even threw down a last-minute dunk for good measure.

For the second consecutive season, the Sixers have a 3-0 start. They got to four wins last year before dropping their first game.

#2838. Nissan cuts sales forecast again on China woes as CEO outlines plan to fight back

Nissan Motor plans to expand exports of vehicles developed in China to help compete against Chinese rivals globally, CEO Ivan Espinosa told CNBC on Wednesday.

The export push comes as intensifying competition in the world's largest auto market forced the company to cut its annual sales forecast for a second time in months.

Espinosa said Nissan would rapidly increase its exports of China-developed vehicles from two models to five, targeting 12 regional market clusters in the "very near term."

The Japanese automaker is responding to intensifying competition in China by developing vehicles specifically for Chinese consumers, using local technology, lower costs and faster development processes, he said.

"We can defend ourselves from these very aggressive players by using exactly the same elements that they have: speed, cost, and good technology," he told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe."

Nissan faces weakening demand and an accelerating shift toward electric vehicles in China, even as its restructuring efforts show progress in the U.S. and Japan.

On Wednesday, Nissan cut its global sales outlook for the fiscal year ending March 2027 to between 2.97 million and 3 million vehicles, down from 3.15 million previously. It maintained its full-year operating profit and net cash guidance.

In China, "weaker demand, a faster-than-anticipated shift to [new energy vehicles] and intensifying competition are putting greater pressure on sales than initially anticipated," the company said in a statement.

In August, Nissan revised its full-year sales volume outlook from 3.3 million units to 3.15 million units.

In October, shares of peer Volvo Cars crashed after it reported a swing to operating loss, citing tough market conditions in China and the U.S.

CEO Håkan Samuelsson told CNBC at the time that it needed a new strategy in China, with regionally adapted cars.

"It's really a lot about the Chinese market, of course, where the change has come faster than we thought, and really now we can see that selling global cars developed in Europe and China will not work, and then this is affecting all the Western players in China," Samuelsson said.

On Wednesday, Nissan also reported a 96% drop in second-quarter operating profit to 2.1 billion yen ($13 million), significantly below analyst expectations of 54.9 billion yen, according to FactSet.

The drop in profits was due to no longer having the benefit of lower U.S. emissions-related expenses that boosted profits a year ago, the company said.

Net revenue grew 8.2% to 3.1 trillion yen in the quarter, largely in line with consensus.

The quarterly results came after the Tokyo market closed. Shares are down 29% year-to-date.

Nissan's Frankfurt-listed shares dropped 4% in early afternoon trading.

Espinosa, who has led the company since April 2025, also emphasised progress on a turnaround plan to reduce its global manufacturing footprint and cut its workforce by 15%. About 18 months into the plan, the company has achieved 365 billion yen in savings, Espinosa said.

#2837. The new records set by Toyota's Hypercar trio with WEC 2026 title

Sebastien Buemi, Brendon Hartley and Ryo Hirakawa had already established their credentials in sportscar racing, but their names were etched into history last week following their success in the 2026 World Endurance Championship.

The Toyota trio were crowned the 2026 Hypercar champions at the Monza 6 Hours finale, following a season in which the Japanese manufacturer comfortably outshone the competition both on track and at the pitwall.

Buemi, Hartley and Hirakawa - aboard the #8 TR010 Hybrid - capitalised on the team's improved form, eventually claiming the title by 18 points after winning three of the eight rounds.

For Buemi and Hartley, the result marked another milestone in their already illustrious careers. Both are now five-time Hypercar/LMP1 champions, making them the most successful drivers by far in WEC history. The closest anyone comes to them in terms of titles is incidentally their team-mate Hirakawa, who clinched his third crown in five years at an incredible strike rate of 60%.

Winning five titles in any world championship is a huge achievement. In fact, Buemi and Hartley are now in a very exclusive club of drivers to have won five or more titles in FIA-sanctioned championships.

Sebastien Loeb - 9 titles (2004-12 in WRC)Sebastien Ogier - 9 titles (2013-18, 2020, 2021, 2025 in WRC)Michael Schumacher - 7 titles (1994-95, 2000-04 in Formula 1)Lewis Hamilton - 7 titles (2008, 2014-15, 2017-20 in Formula 1)Juan Manuel Fangio - 5 titles (1951, 1954-57 in Formula 1)Jose Maria Lopez - 5 titles (2014-16 in WTCC, 2020-21 WEC)

For Buemi, the result carried extra significance, having been the longest-serving driver at Toyota. Since joining its LMP1 attack in 2013, he has witnessed all the highs and lows at Cologne first hand and taken WEC's overall race wins record with 29.

"It's hard to describe the feeling. Obviously, amazing to be sharing it with Brendon and Ryo," he said. "Clearly, losing Le Mans was hard, but then winning the championship makes up for it a bit. I think that win in Fuji put us really into a championship contention and then again that amazing race in Barcelona.

"A bit obviously sad to not be able to fight [at Monza]. We were just too slow, which is a bit sad, after dominating Barcelona. But ultimately we knew coming here we had to beat car #7. We had a decent lead, but you never know with those VSCs and full course yellows what can happen, so it's difficult to realise.

"I think the team really deserves it a lot. It's been amazing for the team having to do this new car and coming here and really winning in front of everyone this time. Nobody can say we won alone, so it's an amazing feeling."

For Hartley, this was his third title with Toyota after taking the 2015 and 2017 championships at Porsche. But after the German marque's withdrawal from the top echelon of sportscar racing, Hartley endured an ill-fated stint in F1 with Toro Rosso before returning to his roots with Toyota in 2019.

"I'm really enjoying it now with Brendon," added Buemi. "We were joking about it because he's younger than me [Buemi is 38, Hartley is 37], so he's a younger five-time world champion. The youngest ever! But it's a pleasure to be sharing the car with my two team-mates and Ryo obviously having the F1 race last week [in Mexico] and doing such a good job again. It's amazing."

#8 Toyota Gazoo Racing Toyota TR010: Sebastien Buemi, Brendon Hartley, Ryo Hirakawa

Following the title triumph, an emotional Hartley opened up about the impact of losing this year's Le Mans 24 Hours, admitting he had taken the defeat personally. Both TR010 Hybrids were running neck-to-neck on Sunday morning at Circuit de la Sarthe, after passing the #12 Cadillac V-Series.R of Norman Nato, when a full-course yellow changed the complexion of the race.

"There were real mixed emotions at Le Mans, especially for me," he said. "When you have a chance to win Le Mans and it slips through your fingers, it is hard to take. Of course, we were really happy for the team that managed to win Le Mans again [with the #7 car] with all this competition.

"But I even took it a bit personally, actually, because it was a stint that I was in the car that I took a bit longer to get by the Cadillac, which allowed the sister car to jump us at the next pit sequence. So I somehow took the responsibility for that.

"Even if for a big part of the race we were dominant, it slipped away. I think we all took it badly, but I personally took a bit of responsibility, and I'm really proud of how the three of us fought back this last part of the season."

Hirakawa's title coronation followed a week after he enjoyed a surprise opportunity to make his F1 debut at the Mexico Grand Prix, where he scored a point for Haas. Promoted from Toyota's Super GT and Super Formula roster, Hirakawa has steadily picked up his pace since his debut in 2022, and has now shown he can outperform his more experienced team-mates.

It says a lot about Hirakawa's talent that Toyota hasn't been able to identify another Japanese driver who could take over when Kamui Kobayashi steps down from driving duties in the #7 car.

Ryo Hirakawa, Haas F1 Team, Laura Mueller, Haas F1 Team

"I'm just really happy, not only for car eight, but I think I can say that this is the biggest achievement for Toyota Motorsport," said Hirakawa. "We won the Le Mans, and winning the championship for both drivers and manufacturers, I think it's a massive achievement for us.

"Big thanks to the team, and everyone did a great job. We had a really tough year last year, but we came with a new package, and we won everything, so we need to be proud of that. At the same time, we want to be even stronger for next year.

"To be honest, I can't wait for next year, but I just wanted to appreciate everyone involved. I can't wait for next year."

To read more Motorsport.com articles visit our website.

#2836. Cleveland Browns news: A big nothingburger at the trade deadline

It was a quiet day for the Cleveland Browns as the NFL trade deadline came and went on Tuesday afternoon.

Whether or not general manager Andrew Berry tried to bring anyone on board to help keep the team's playoff hopes alive is unknown.

And while there was speculation that the Minnesota Vikings, who were desperately in need of anyone with a pulse to play quarterback, might come calling about the greatest backup quarterback in NFL history, that did not come to pass as the Vikings instead decided to reunite with Kirk Cousins after three years apart.

Minnesota is returning to Cousins after cycling through eight different starting quarterbacks since the beginning of the 2024 season. If he starts this week against Green Bay, it will be the 15th time the Vikings have changed the starting quarterback from the previous week since moving on from Cousins.

Those numbers highlight something that has been evident for a long time now. While the Browns get more than their share of grief for continually messing up the quarterback position, they are far from being alone in the league in that regard.

And speaking of the Browns and quarterbacks, maybe today will be the day that head coach Todd Monken stops playing silly games and lets everyone know who the starting quarterback will be for this week's game against the Houston Texans.

#2835. Paul George flips the script — and the bird — in Philadelphia return

Paul George flips the script — and the bird — in Philadelphia return originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

PG-13 was rated R in his return to Philadelphia.

The middle finger Paul George directed toward one baseline fan only reinforced that rating on a night he poured in 36 points.

Tuesday night was expected to revolve around Jaylen Brown and Jayson Tatum sharing an NBA court as opponents for the first time. Instead, the other prominent player in the summer's blockbuster trade stole the show.

George scored a game-high 36 points in Boston's 123-101 win over the Sixers. He shot 14 for 22 from the floor, made six of his 11 three-point attempts and scored 14 consecutive Boston points in the fourth quarter.

The game was tied at 79 with 3:19 remaining in the third quarter. Boston outscored Philadelphia 44-22 from that point forward.

The Celtics went nine minutes and 20 seconds without making a two-point field goal during the second half. Instead, they made nine consecutive field goals from three, the second-longest such streak by a Sixers opponent over the last 30 seasons.

Payton Pritchard opened the fourth with consecutive threes. George followed with eight straight points, helping the Celtics begin the quarter on a 14-0 run that turned a competitive game into a rout.

George's return carried a complicated history. His two seasons with the Sixers were repeatedly interrupted by injuries, but he also played a significant role in their most memorable playoff run in years.

Last spring, George helped the Sixers overcome a 3-1 deficit to eliminate Boston in seven games. He averaged 17.4 points and shot 55 percent from three during the series.

Tyrese Maxey was happy to see his former teammate Tuesday, even if he disliked what followed.

"It was good to see Paul," Maxey said. "I love him, love his family. … He gave us everything he had while he was here. We know what he did in the playoffs last year. Great person, great family, and we'll see him again."

Maxey then added one important qualifier.

George instead scored from nearly everywhere.

"He had some open threes. He had some pull-up threes. He had some middies," Maxey said. "He played within the game, too. That's what Paul does. He plays within the game. He takes what the defense gives him, and we gave him too many open looks tonight.

"PG, we know what PG does. We shouldn't even be allowing him to get up 11 threes, and that's on us."

Nick Nurse watched George attack the Sixers in several ways.

"He really had it going," Nurse said. "He'd post up, and-one, fallaways. He went down the lane, high archer off the glass, and-one. He scored in about every way he could tonight."

George heard boos during his return and said the arena carried the atmosphere, though not quite the intensity, of a playoff game.

"This building can be very electric as well," George said. "It was fun. It was fun to play in this environment."

One interaction extended beyond the usual reception for a returning player.

During a stoppage, the ball rolled toward a fan seated along the baseline. George said the fan looked directly at him before dropping the ball instead of returning it.

"There was a fan being an a–," George said. "The ball rolled to him. He looked me dead in my eyes, dropped the ball on the ground as if I'm a dog to go fetch or go get it, instead of passing the ball to me like a normal fan would do.

"And so, yeah, then I rolled him a middle finger in return."

#2834. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

#2833. General strikes to paralyze Spain as protestors clash with police ahead of election campaign

Spanish workers are expected to turn out in the millions on Wednesday for the country's first general strike in 14 years, as anger over soaring rents and living costs intensifies less than three weeks before a general election. Demonstrators clashed with police at a wholesale market in Madrid in the early hours of the strike, while roadblocks were also reported in the early hours of Wednesday. The 24-hour labor walkout is expected to disrupt public transport and flights, as well as bring some businesses, shops and factories to a standstill.

Housing costs are a particular source of frustration among demonstrators. Rents have almost doubled over the past decade, squeezing household budgets as many workers feel homeownership is beyond their reach. The Bank of Spain estimates that the country faces a shortage of 750,000 homes, potentially rising to 1 million over the next two years. Spain's two largest trade unions, CCOO and UGT, which called the strike, are calling for greater government intervention in the housing market. Housing activists demand an end to tourist rentals and automatic lease renewals, while unions are seeking greater housing intervention and a new minimum-wage agreement.

UGT Secretary-General Pepe Álvarez urged members to join the protests, saying "there is no salary that can cope with the price of housing in this country," in comments translated by CNBC. He added that it was time for the government to listen to citizens' demands.

The strike follows weeks of social unrest. The eviction of an 87-year-old woman from her Madrid home, followed by her death around two weeks later, sparked national outrage. The left-wing government's attempts to pass measures to help tenants were blocked by the opposition.

Prime Minister Pedro Sánchez's coalition responded by calling an election for Nov. 29, hoping to bolster his support and secure another four years in power. But a high-speed train collision that killed 69 people, the second such disaster this year, shifted the debate toward public safety and accusations of government mismanagement.

The government has not officially endorsed the strike, whose slogan is "Salaries. Housing. Prices." But the protests are widely seen as an opportunity for left-wing parties, including Sánchez's Spanish Socialist Workers' Party, to put housing at the center of the election campaign. Political scientist Oriol Bartomeus of the Autonomous University of Barcelona told The Associated Press that a strong, peaceful turnout could help the left return the campaign to a focus on housing. Sánchez took to social media earlier this week to voice support for existing policies including national health insurance and a 40-hour workweek, warning that a right-wing government would introduce austerity measures and cuts to social services.

"Your eight-hour working day. Your pensions. Your public healthcare. None of this fell from the sky. They were collective achievements," he told voters on Monday.

The conservative Popular Party and far-right Vox, which already govern in some parts of Spain, accuse the unions of organizing the protests to keep the current government in power. "The strike is not a general strike, it's an election strike," Miguel Tellado, a spokesperson for the Popular Party, said Tuesday.

Both parties oppose intervention in the housing market.

Opinion polls suggest a shift to the right, with the Popular Party leading and potentially able to form a government with Vox.

Such a coalition would bring the far right into Spain's national government for the first time since Francisco Franco's 36-year dictatorship ended in 1975.

A poll for El País and Cadena SER, published Nov. 9, put the Popular Party at 31.9%, Sánchez's Partido Socialista Obrero Español at 29.9% and Vox at 18%. The survey, conducted Nov. 2-4, projected a combined 190 seats for the Popular Party and Vox, above the 176 needed for a majority in the 350-seat lower house. A strong turnout at Wednesday's protests could prove crucial for the government, whose handling of the migration crisis in Ceuta drew widespread criticism. Sánchez's family has also been plagued by corruption scandals.

The prime minister's personal standing has suffered. Some 58% of respondents had a "bad" or "very bad" opinion of him, compared with 41% who had a good or very good opinion, according to a poll for elDiario.es conducted Nov. 2-5.

Violence during the strike, however, could backfire on the government. Protests in France in October were seen as an opportunity for the country's far-right party to emphasize its ability to restore order and security ahead of the country's 2027 presidential elections.

"On the evening of 11 November, there will almost certainly be two narratives: one of resounding success and one of failure. Neither should be read as an exit poll. It will be more useful to look at other things: which sectors and regions stop work, how many people take to the streets and, above all, whether housing moves to the centre of the campaign," wrote Maite Aurrekoetxea, professor of social and human sciences at the University of Deusto, in comments translated by Google.

#2832. Opponent Breakdown: North Carolina Tar Heels Offense

Bill Belichick had a poor start to his tenure at UNC last year, with the main issues being on the offensive side of the ball. He remedied that issue this year by going out and hiring none other than Bobby Petrino to be his offensive coordinator. Petrino had been bouncing around at a few spots since being fired by Louisville, but his offensive acumen is still coveted. The Tar Heels have improved on offense, though they still rank toward the bottom of the rankings in some key offensive metrics.

As always, an offense is only as good as its quarterback, and the Tar Heels are average with Billy Edwards under center. The veteran transfer quarterback has played about as well as most expected him to this season. His inconsistent play has been an issue for this offense, and it has contributed to a couple of the losses this year.

As the season has gone on, he has played better, with his last two games being his best. He torched Miami's defense for four touchdowns while completing 82% of his passes. He came back the following weekend to complete 76% of his passes against a solid UConn team. Edwards isn't one of the top quarterbacks Louisville has seen this season, but he has played like one recently.

UNC has three running backs that they can rely on, and they play all three of them. Demon June leads the group in touchdowns with 8 on the season. Benjamin Hall leads the group in yards and yards per carry. Jaylin Mcgill leads the group in yards per play as he is a factor as a receiver out of the backfield. They are all over 220 pounds, and Petrino has done a good job of utilizing them this season. The staff likes to ride the hot hand with this group, so we will have to wait to see which player gets the most touches.

Louisville will face another opponent that has a single key player at the wide receiver position this weekend. Jordan Shipp is one of the more talented players the Cards will face this year, and they will need to have a plan. Shipp has good size at 6-2/200, and he can really move. He is a fluid athlete with good body control which he uses to make contested catches and catches in tight spots. He is not a burner in the open field, but he has plenty of speed to score on a long play if he gets open.

The biggest threat on this offense is at tight end in Jelani Thurman. Lincoln Keinholz' former teammate at Ohio State has had a similar situation as he did not make much of an impact for the Buckeyes, but he has emerged as one of the best tight ends in the country for UNC. He has already set the school record for touchdowns by a tight end with 8, which is the most in the country among tight ends. He also ranks in the top five in just about every receiving category when it comes to tight ends.

This team doesn't create a lot of big passing plays, but when they do it is down the middle of the field to Thurman, or because he pulls defenders with him. Petrino is known for working deep over routes throughout his career, but this team has killed teams down the seams. Louisville's defense has been victimized many times on seam routes this year, so they will need to adjust their plan from their normal reliance on Cover 3.

The UNC offensive line has been pretty average this year, especially from a run blocking standpoint. They don't allow a lot of penetration, but they haven't been opening massive holes for the running backs, either. Where they have struggled is being consistent in pass protection. Billy Edwards is just a big better than being a statue in the pocket, so he is not one to avoid the rush if it gets to him. It has not helped that his line has struggled with speed rushers this year. The Cards should have an opportunity to get pressure this weekend.

#2831. LeBron Log No. 4: A courtside sip, 16,000 makes and Cleveland's welcome

LeBron Log No. 4: A courtside sip, 16,000 makes and Cleveland's welcome originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

LeBron James' latest week with the Sixers included a sip of a stranger's wine, yet another number no NBA player had reached and a return to the city where his career began.

It also included his worst performance with Philadelphia, followed less than 48 hours later by his highest-scoring game with the team.

Welcome to the fourth edition of the LeBron Log.

No chronicle of James' first season in Philadelphia could omit the time he helped himself to a fan's wine during a game.

James chased Tyrese Maxey's transition pass into the stands during the third quarter of the Sixers' Nov. 2 win over the Bulls. He collided with Philadelphia native Troy Clemons, spilled some of Clemons' wine and sampled what had landed on his arm.

The interaction received its own story on our site, and deservedly so. It also belongs in the Log.

Clemons did not keep the cup as a souvenir. He threw it away and got another drink.

James returned to the court and later broke into a dance after scoring over the 6-foot-1 Tre Jones. The wine clip generated millions of views across social media and became the defining image from an otherwise ordinary 19-point Sixers victory.

The following night in Detroit, James offered a broader explanation for why Philadelphia has already looked comfortable together.

"We've got a good team," James said. "Guys vibe with one another. Guys like each other. Guys enjoy being around each other. That has a lot to do with it, too. It makes the game a lot easier when you enjoy being around people."

Eleven games into his Sixers career, James appears to be doing exactly that.

James arrived in Detroit two baskets away from another unprecedented number.

His second field goal against the Pistons made him the first player in NBA history to reach 16,000 regular-season field goals.

James finished the Sixers' 112-106 victory with 13 points, 10 rebounds and eight assists. The near-triple-double helped Philadelphia improve to 8-1 and avenge its only loss of the season.

Kareem Abdul-Jabbar ranks second in league history with 15,837 made field goals. Karl Malone is a distant third at 13,528. James is the only active player with more than 12,000.

His scoring record has long been secure. James passed Abdul-Jabbar in career points in February 2023 and later overtook him in made baskets. The round number in Detroit still illustrated the distance he has created between himself and every other scorer.

James began his career by making 5,361 field goals across seven seasons with Cleveland. He added 3,510 in Miami, returned to make 3,691 more for the Cavaliers and made another 3,421 during eight years with the Lakers.

Basket No. 16,000 belonged to Philadelphia.

The Sixers' 8-1 start also carried a notable James connection. Each of his previous four teams to begin a season at least 7-1 reached the NBA Finals.

James was not ready to entertain that conversation in early November.

"It's too early to be talking about the Finals and things of that nature," he said. "We've got to continue to buy into what we want to do."

Two nights later, James returned to the place where nearly half of those 16,000 baskets were made.

His first game in Cleveland as a Sixer brought out jerseys from every stop of his career. Cavaliers colors filled Rocket Arena alongside Heat black, Lakers purple and the Sixers' throwback look.

James received a warm ovation when he was introduced last among Philadelphia's starters. When he checked out late in the fourth quarter, with the result decided, the crowd stood for him again.

James has experienced both ends of Cleveland's emotional range.

He returned as a member of the Heat on Dec. 2, 2010, to one of the most hostile receptions in NBA history. Six years later, he delivered the Cavaliers their first championship and ended Cleveland's 52-year major professional sports title drought.

"I've seen the depths of hell when it comes to a reception here, all the way to the heavens above," James said. "Anything in between is easy money."

The reception fell somewhere in the middle. His performance landed much closer to the bottom.

James finished with eight points on 3-for-12 shooting, three assists and six turnovers in the Sixers' 119-95 loss. It was his first single-digit performance in Cleveland since he scored three points against the Rockets on Dec. 30, 2004.

He had scored at least 10 points in 396 consecutive regular-season games in the city. The run covered his first stint with the Cavaliers, every visit as an opponent and his entire second tenure with the organization.

It ended during his first trip back in a Sixers uniform.

"I definitely did not feel in a good rhythm offensively," James said. "A couple of them were careless. A couple of them were just trying to make the right play and not being successful with it."

It was only the 10th single-digit regular-season game of James' 1,630-game career. He still took time afterward to consider what an eventual jersey retirement in Cleveland could represent.

"The journey from being an 18-year-old rookie, to leading a franchise to a championship," James said. "And everything in between."

James did not carry the rough night into Chicago.

Less than 48 hours after struggling in Cleveland, he scored a season-high 22 points in the Sixers' 127-121 victory over the Bulls. Thirteen came during the second quarter, when James helped Philadelphia turn an early deficit into a halftime lead.

His first basket produced another milestone.

James entered Sunday trailing Abdul-Jabbar's 1,895 points for the most scored against the Bulls in NBA history. His first-quarter three-pointer moved him into the lead. He ended the afternoon with 1,916.

The most interesting play from his night was a pass.

With the Sixers ahead by four and 2:20 remaining, James drove into the paint. Three Chicago defenders collapsed toward him. Maxey stood between the left wing and corner, but even he did not believe James could see him.

James fired the ball through the defense. Maxey sidestepped Isaac Okoro and made the three-pointer, pushing Philadelphia's lead to seven.

After his postgame interview, James pulled out his phone and watched the play again.

"He didn't think I saw him," James called out from his locker as reporters asked Maxey about the possession.

Maxey acknowledged that he had positioned himself to rebound because he expected James to shoot.

"Honestly, I was like this," Maxey said, raising his hands. "I thought he was about to shoot a layup."

#2829. American soldier arrested on suspicion of spying for Russia

A senior soldier in the U.S. Army has been arrested in the U.K. on suspicion of spying for Russia, London's Metropolitan Police said on Tuesday evening.

The unnamed 41-year-old man was arrested near Gloucester, a city in western England, on Tuesday as part of an investigation by the U.K.'s Counter Terrorism Policing. British police worked with U.S. authorities, including the military and the FBI, in their investigations.

A senior NATO military official confirmed on Tuesday that the American service member was assigned to the military alliance's Allied Rapid Reaction Corps (ARRC).

ARRC, headquartered in the English county of Gloucestershire, is a  3-star NATO headquarters with more than 400 permanent staff from more than 20 nations ready to deploy land forces where they are needed.

Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov told reporters on Wednesday that Moscow had "absolutely nothing to do with" the case in the U.K.

The individual at the center of the case, detained in London, is suspected of committing offenses under Sections 1 and 3 of the U.K.'s National Security Act.

Section 1 offenses relate to obtaining or disclosing protected information, while Section 3 involves assisting a foreign intelligence service.

The maximum penalty is life imprisonment for a Section 1 offense and 14 years' imprisonment for a Section 3 offense. A fine may also be imposed for either offense.

Police officers are carrying out searches at two locations in Gloucestershire, the Metropolitan Police added.

The arrest comes as Washington is seeking to broker an end to Russia's war in Ukraine. U.S.-mediated talks between Russian and Ukrainian officials began in Abu Dhabi on Tuesday, the first such meeting since February, while Kyiv and its European allies continue to press for credible security guarantees as part of any settlement.

President Donald Trump, a longstanding critic of NATO and its members' defense budgets, said on Monday that there appeared to be a growing willingness on both sides to end the almost five-year war.

Russia has long threatened retaliatory action against an expansion of NATO's eastern flank and has previously demanded guarantees that Ukraine will never be allowed to join the alliance. Finland and Sweden became NATO's newest members in 2023 and 2024, respectively.

Since Russia and Ukraine have been at war, Russian activity has also been reported deeper into European and NATO territory, with regional authorities investigating whether Moscow could be involved in some of the recent disarray seen across the continent.

FBI Director Kash Patel said in a statement on Tuesday evening that the allegations against a U.S. serviceman were "of the utmost concern to the United States and our allies."

"I want to send a clear message – this FBI has no tolerance for anyone who breaks their oath to the United States and whose actions endanger our national security and that of our partners," he said.

Sean Stinchon, the commanding general of U.S. Army Counterintelligence Command, said in a statement that "the alleged conduct of a senior solider, entrusted with sensitive information, is a sobering reminder that insider threats are real and persistent."

Authorities said Tuesday that the investigation is not related to any other ongoing Counter Terrorism Policing investigations.

On Oct. 27, British prosecutors said they had charged a man with assisting a foreign intelligence service over an incident at a military base used by the U.S. for strikes on Iran. The base in question, RAF Fairford, is in Gloucestershire, around 30 miles from ARRC's headquarters at Imjin Barracks.

#2827. Bessent reportedly moved to military base over Iranian threats on his life

U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent was reportedly moved from his Washington, D.C., home to a military base due to Iranian threats on his life.

The New York Times, citing officials familiar with the matter, said that U.S. officials had concluded Iranian plotting against current and former American officials had reached a more advanced stage.

Separately, Reuters reported that the severity of the threats against Bessent was not immediately clear.

Bessent has led Operation Economic Outcast, a Treasury campaign launched in August to isolate Iran economically through sanctions and other financial measures, in addition to the U.S. military actions.

The Times reported that Bessent's role in that campaign has made him one of the Trump administration officials Iran most wanted to target.

CNBC has contacted the White House and the U.S. Department of the Treasury for comment.

The move comes as the Department of Justice said late Tuesday that three Iranian nationals had been indicted on terrorism-related charges.

The department said the three were allegedly involved in nearly 20 attacks and attempted attacks across Europe and the United States.

The alleged plots included a September 2026 attack targeting RAF Fairford in the U.K., where U.S. military personnel were stationed.

"Anyone who plans a terrorist plot on a military base housing American service members will be prosecuted by the United States government," Attorney General Todd Blanche said.

Earlier in October, British Prime Minister Andy Burnham said Iran was involved in the incident at RAF Fairford that led to the arrest of five men on suspicion of explosives and terrorism offenses.

Iranian Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi then rejected the allegation, writing on X that Burnham was "barking up the wrong tree."

RAF Fairford, in Gloucestershire, is a key U.S. Air Force operating location in Europe and has supported deployments of American strategic bombers and other aircraft.

Separately, U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio reportedly said military action against Iran may not be necessary if economic sanctions and Washington's naval blockade are working.

On Monday, President Donald Trump said on Truth Social that "Qatar, Saudi Arabia, UAE, Bahrain, Kuwait, and others, would prefer the status quo, in other words, a continuation of the Greatest Naval Blockade in History."

"We are seeing positive momentum toward an end to this Conflict," Trump added.

Asked Tuesday why he would not simply declare an end to the Iran war, Trump said he wanted to ensure that U.S. allies in the region were "not hurt" and that there was a "long-term solution.

However, Iranian officials have denied that negotiations are underway. Security chief Mohsen Rezaei said claims that talks were continuing were aimed at "easing economic pressures and reducing oil prices."

Brent and West Texas Intermediate crude prices edged higher during Wednesday's Asia trading session, at about $92 and $86 per barrel, respectively.

#2826. Observations after Sixers lose 1st Brown vs. Tatum matchup, George scores 36

Observations after Sixers lose 1st Brown vs. Tatum matchup, George scores 36 originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Round 1 of the new-look Sixers vs. the new-look Celtics decisively went Boston's way on Tuesday night.

In the first meeting between the teams since the July trade that sent Jaylen Brown to Philadelphia and Paul George to Boston, the Celtics earned a 123-101 win at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

The Sixers dropped to 9-3 and the Celtics improved to 9-2.

George was the game's brightest star, scoring 36 points on 14-for-22 shooting. Jayson Tatum added 21 points.

Tyrese Maxey's 24 points led the Sixers. Brown was hampered by foul trouble in his heavily anticipated first outing against Tatum. He posted 13 points, five rebounds and two assists in 26 minutes.

The Sixers will host the Raptors on Thursday night. Here are observations on their blowout loss to the Celtics:

To no one's surprise, Brown and Tatum guarded each other to open the night.

VJ Edgecombe defended a former teammate in George, who scored Boston's first four points with a pair of pure jumpers. On the other end, Maxey made the Sixers' first three field goals.

Judging by volume, the home fans cared most about the much-scrutinized Brown vs. Tatum matchup. The crowd was excited early when Brown snagged a steal and then dished to Maxey for a fast-break three-pointer. The fans also dialed up the noise when Brown sized up Tatum in isolation. He wound up dribbling the ball off his foot and out of bounds. Tatum pointed to signal it was Celtics ball.

Boston's star forward had a productive first quarter, posting 11 points. He scored an easy two when he used a back screen to break free from Brown and convert a layup that put the Celtics up 11-7. Boston began 9 for 12 from the floor as a team.

As for Brown, he sat shortly after being called for his second foul with 6:48 left in the first quarter.

Brown re-entered at the start of the second quarter. The Sixers still struggled to eat into Boston's lead.

A Sam Hauser triple extended the Celtics' advantage to 12 points. There was some audible frustration in the building after Brown missed two free throws on the next possesion.

The Sixers' deficit hit 16 points on a Hugo Gonzalez jumper. Meanwhile, the Sixers missed 12 of their first 14 three-point tries. While some of those shots were open looks that simply didn't go down, the Sixers also rushed up several contested jumpers before they made Boston's defense do serious work.

In the bigger picture, it wouldn't be remotely surprising if shooting stands out as a weak area for the Sixers come the trade deadline. They entered the game ranked 27th in the NBA in three-point frequency outside of garbage time and 22nd in three-point accuracy, according to Cleaning the Glass.

Joel Embiid (16 points, six rebounds) had an injury scare late in the second quarter when he fell hard after missing a layup in transition. He rose gingerly and limped through the final 30 seconds or so of the first half.

The Sixers' star big man was fine to start the third quarter and even drained a long-overdue three on the opening possession. He'd been in a terrible outside shooting slump (3 for 32 from deep on the season) before that shot.

Brown's foul trouble persisted. He picked up his fourth personal with 10:40 to go in the third quarter.

The Sixers stormed back without him and their defense played a large role. After LeBron James rumbled in for a layup, Dean Wade stonewalled Payton Pritchard on the perimeter to force a shot-clock violation. Wade later canned a three to even the contest at 76-all.

The Celtics responded and Tatum led the charge. His third-quarter exploits included a highlight slam on James and a top-of-the-key three to build Boston's lead back up to nine points.

Pritchard buried a three on the first possession of the fourth quarter and the Celtics' advantage kept growing. George celebrated a triple that stretched his team's lead to 100-82. The Sixers' spirits were considerably lower as they headed to their bench for a timeout. As a Sixer, George only had one higher-scoring game (39 points) than his performance Tuesday.

George and Tatum were each excellent in Philadelphia. Outside of Maxey, who finished 8 for 12 from the field, the Sixers didn't receive similar games from their stars.

Brown will be back in Boston on Jan. 21 when the Sixers visit the Celtics. He'll try to even the score.

#2825. Nvidia's Huang tries to project 'business as usual' in return to office after affair revelation

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang walked into the chipmaker's Santa Clara, California, headquarters on Monday and conducted his regular morning meeting with top-level executives, people familiar with the meeting told CNBC.

On Sunday, Huang had disclosed an extramarital affair to employees.

Huang kicked off the closed-door gathering by addressing his infidelity and sharing how sorry he was to his staff, according to the people, who asked not to be named because they were not authorized to speak publicly. They said Huang didn't take any questions and quickly pivoted to business and top agenda items of the day.

The CEO was also in the office on Tuesday, conducting meetings just one week ahead of the company's quarterly earnings report.

Nvidia did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

People inside the artificial intelligence chip giant said Huang tried to project "business as usual," even as the probe moved forward into his affair with an alleged Chinese spy. The CEO was in a romantic relationship with Alice Huang, a former Megaspeed executive, according to the Financial Times.

Sentiment toward Jensen Huang shifted on Sunday after the 63-year-old revealed in an email to employees that he had an affair, though he did not disclose with whom he had the relationship.

"I know an apology cannot undo what I have done," he wrote. "I hope our family will heal and that, over time, I can earn back your trust." Nvidia's board opened an investigation and hired Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to conduct the probe on Tuesday.

Employees inside the firm described the mood to CNBC as "sad." One employee told CNBC that they were shocked by Jensen Huang's revelations.

People also told CNBC that longtime employees viewed Jensen Huang as an idol who could do no wrong and thought of him as a guiding light. Many employees at the company are also struggling to come to grips with the CEO's actions, the people said.

Jensen Huang co-founded Nvidia in 1993 to build graphics processing units for video game graphics. In recent years, the company has become synonymous with the AI boom, powering the technology that underpins large language models and the massive data center buildout.

A central figure in the development of every AI layer, the Nvidia CEO is also a key voice in Washington, even accompanying President Donald Trump on his trip to China in May. In September, Jensen Huang and his wife were pictured at the same table as Chinese President Xi Jinping for the White House's state dinner.

The news of the CEO's affair has drawn increased scrutiny on his continued support for more AI chip sales to China.

It was a little surprising to see a fourth-round pick involved, though Baltimore got a fifth-round pick back. The Ravens got center Cam Jurgens weeks ago, but that came at the hefty price of a second-round pick to Philadelphia.

#2823. What Kirby Smart said about Missouri football

The Georgia Bulldogs are preparing to host the Missouri Tigers as Georgia looks to start a new home winning streak after losing their last home game to the Auburn Tigers. Georgia is favored by over two touchdowns against Missouri and is looking to get one step closer to advancing to the final SEC championship game.

"We are moving to Missouri," Georgia coach Kirby Smart said at his weekly press conference. "I have a lot of respect for Eli (Drinkwitz). He is one of my close friends. I think he is one of the best coaches in the country. He is very charismatic. He cares about the game. He cares about the SEC. He cares about these players. He wants the game to be better, and he has been very vocal on a lot of SEC and national issues that I've been involved in with him. He does a lot for the game. He does a tremendous job as a coach.

"I think this is one of his better teams. He has got an elite quarterback, and he's got good talent surrounding him. He is playing in a tough, brutal league just like we all are, and he has been in some really tight ball games. He has had some close calls, and they've got a tremendous team. They're playing really well on defense and offense. It will be a great challenge for us. I'm glad we get to come home."

Georgia (8-1, 6-1 SEC) enters the Missouri game as one of the hottest teams in the country. The Bulldogs earned a 27-8 win over Florida and a 42-7 win over Ole Miss in the two games after Georgia's bye week. Missouri (6-3, 3-3 SEC) is coming off a tough home loss to the Texas Longhorns and still has an outside shot to make the College Football Playoff, but the Tigers would have to win out.

"His accuracy, his arm strength, and his anticipation of throws," Smart said. "I am a big baseball fan, and he is an elite baseball player and pitcher. His throws are different. They get there really fast. He can whip it out. He can throw it anywhere.

"I think we all saw the throw he made a couple of weeks ago over the middle. His touch, his timing. He can do it all. He is a really good athlete, so he is in a good system with a tremendous arm with really good wideouts around him. They have done a great job in the run game. They have always done a great job in the run game. I have a lot of respect for him. He came in against us (in 2024) and played with a lot of confidence there at Ole Miss."

"They've got a unique run game," Smart said. "They've got some run game that other teams in the conference don't utilize and don't use as much. They've always done that. Drink (Eli Drinkwitz) has always done that. Whoever his offensive coordinator has been, he's been very involved in that part of the offense.

"They have a commitment to the run, which is why they run the ball well... He's committed to being stubborn with it. It doesn't allow you to rush the passer first. It helps your passing game which they have a really good passing game. Their commitment to the run game and downhill runs helps them be who they are. They've done a tremendous job of it. They've got really good backs. They've got a great system."

Missouri running back Jamal Roberts has 209 carries, which is the most in the country (tied). The Tigers are committed to establishing the run and have All-SEC star Ahmad Hardy back.

"Physical on the front," Smart said. "They have one of the most disruptive fronts in the league. They've got interior D-linemen with six and seven sacks. They've got edge guys with six and seven sacks. They've got speed, size.

"They play really hard. They know who they are. They have an identity. Just a lot of respect for how they play, and that's how you stop a run because you have a mindset. They've got one. They do a really good job."

"I love Cayden Lee," Smart said. "He's a kid that I feel like I've seen for so long. He trained with (former Georgia receiver Terrence Edwards) and getting to see him kind of grow through those years with him. The guy loves football, and he played so well even the last couple years while at Ole Miss.

"He gave us problems, and then he's had an outstanding year this year. Just incredible. The catches he's made, the tough catches, the meaningful catches, the third down catches. They utilize him really well. They have a really good system to utilize his skill set. Just so impressed with a kid that I watched grow up coming to our camps, and he's just done a tremendous job. I have so much respect for his competitive character and his family. I spoke to him after the Ole Miss game last year. To see what he's done this year, he's been really incredible."

"They've been really elite," Smart said. "They do a great job. They're one of the few teams in the league ahead of us at taking the ball away, and they've also been really good at protecting the ball, as well. They have been elite at that.

"To say it's just the front-four, I wouldn't say that. It's everybody involved. The front-four is where you get your sack fumbles, but those backhand guys are primarily the tacklers and the second-level guys and the punch-out guys."
