#2804. Jaguars GM James Gladstone: We value draft picks, but don't always use them in April

Jaguars General Manager James Gladstone doesn't view this week's moves to acquire safety Jessie Bates, defensive tackle Jordan Davis, and defensive tackle Davon Godchaux in the same light as many others around the football world have characterized them.

Gladstone sent away the team's 2027 first- and second-round picks, 2028 fifth- and sixth-round picks, and 2029 second-round pick in three Monday trades, which has been positioned as going all-in on maximizing the team's Super Bowl chances after a 6-2 start. In a Tuesday press conference, Gladstone said he wouldn't "necessarily" use the same framing and said he'd describe the team's approach as always "getting better as a football team and prioritizing getting better as a football team."

Another popular response has been that Gladstone has adopted his former Rams boss Les Snead's "F them picks" to building the team's Super Bowl LVI winners and subsequent teams. Gladstone noted that the Rams made plenty of picks while he was there from 2017-2024 and that he always takes into account the compensatory draft pick process during free agency as a way to supplement assets that can be used in a variety of ways.

"It's a very symbiotic process that encompasses a lot of layers to it, but it's certainly not 'F them picks,'" Gladstone said. "While that's been characterized as the approach, it's not necessarily — we actually value picks. We don't always use them on the three days that exist in April."

Gladstone also acknowledged that "a part of it" is acquiring known commodities rather than gambling on the unknown trajectories of draft picks. There's a gamble involved with the Jaguars' approach as well and the way it pays off will be a big part of Gladstone's ultimate legacy in Jacksonville.

#2803. Knicks 117, Bucks 85: Scenes from some spicy OG, KAT & Kolek cooking

One can draw conclusions from Milwaukee's 1-10 record. For instance, maybe Giannis Antetokounmpo was even more important to their success than we understood. Maybe we overestimated coach Taylor Jenkins a li'l bit? Or maybe the Deer just stink this season? We remember when Jenkins competed with Mike Brown for the Knicks' top job. There's a sliding doors moment we'd rather not consider.

Tonight, Jenkins got a solid effort from his team in the opening quarter. A regulation game lasts 48 minutes, though, and New York's stifling defense held the Bucks to just 34 points through the first 24. Ahead by two dozen at halftime, the home team let the guests cut the deficit to 11 early in the fourth. Then the dynamic duo of Karl-Anthony Towns and Tyler Kolek restored some sanity, and by the bell, the Knicks had won, 117–85.

For those counting at home, that's four consecutive wins to bring New York to 6-4. It's also the fourth time in five games that they've held an opponent under 100 points. No Knicks starter played more than 30 minutes (nice, because they'll need their legs tomorrow against Cleveland). And that's officially the Bucks' worst start in 33 years.

The Bucks didn't play like a last-place team in the first quarter, nor did the Knicks comport themselves like world champions. The lead changed seven times before New York finally found traction late in the quarter. On the plus side, Landry Shamet hit two treys, and Mikal Bridges chipped in two buckets, three rebounds, a steal and a block. For the villains, Ryan Rollins stood out with five points, two assists, two rebounds and a steal.

The Knicks lead their conference in rebounding percentage (53.3%). Give credit to Karl-Anthony Towns (21 PTS, 16 RBS, 27 MIN), who's grabbing 13.6 boards per night. The KAT campaign for MVP rolled on this evening. In the first quarter, Towns played straight-up defense on one end, deterring a Ryan Rollins drive. At the other end, he played quarterback, hitting Jalen Brunson (23 PTS) on a cut to the cup. He feasted on the well-fed Myles Turner, and the Knicks won the paint 16-6 and the boards 16-8 in the first quarter. The three-ball kept the Bucks competitive, but by the period's end, the hosts were ahead 26-21.

From the "Gotta Complain About Something" file, Anunoby failed to flush the ball twice tonight. He's getting to the charity stripe at a career-high clip, but he's also clanged plenty off the front of the iron. You can't gripe about this stat-line, though: 19 points, 6-of-12 FG, 5-of-8 3PT, and a +26.

After a strong showing in the first quarter, the visitors wilted fast in the second. Milwaukee scored first, then New York rattled off 10 straight to push the lead to a baker's dozen. Tyler Kolek (12 PTS, 2-3 3PT, 15 MIN) capped the run with a three, then blocked an AJ Green three, grabbed the rebound and took it the other way for a layup. After a Jenkins timeout, Kolek turned a missed Jaime Jaquez, Jr. (16 PTS) three into a driving finger roll for two.

A little past midway through the frame, the good guys had outscored Milwaukee 19-5. Towns cleaned the glass, and Andre Drummond (14 RBS, 16 MIN) grabbed nine boards in eight first-half minutes. Three came on the offensive end.

Anunoby had a fine second frame, scoring eight with two threes, two assists and a steal. Brunson chipped in six points, and Towns grabbed five boards and made two free throws. Nate Ament led Milwaukee with five points and added a steal. The Knicks won the frame 32-13 and went into halftime up 58-34.

At the half, Milwaukee had matched New York's eight threes and nearly matched its percentage from deep (35% to 36%). Inside the arc, the Bucks were a dreadful 5-for-19 against the Knicks' 14-for-27. New York also doubled Milwaukee on the boards, 34-17, including 10 offensive rebounds. Anunoby, Brunson and Shamet shared the halftime scoring lead with 10 apiece. Ament had seven for the guests.

Jaquez burst out of intermission with fire and desire, scoring nine of his team's first 15 points as the Bucks lopped 10 points off their deficit. Rollins had a fine quarter, too, hitting three treys. KAT missed his first six tries from deep but still managed his 10th double-double of the season. The Captain found his touch, scoring 13 of the Knicks' 27 third-quarter points. For the visitors, Kasparas Jakučionis added five points and two assists in the final 3:08. The Bucks won the quarter 38-27 and trailed 85-72 heading into the fourth.

After surrendering an alley-oop to Kel'el Ware, the Knicks found themselves ahead by 11 early in the fourth. It was time to get serious. Towns drilled a triple, his first make in seven tries. He missed two freebies, but Shamet grabbed the rebound and Kolek cashed it in from deep. Oh, and don't look now, but here comes Karl with another bomb! He and Kolek synergized and synthesized, combining for New York's first 11 points of the quarter.

Anunoby buried back-to-back threes to cap a 21-6 Knicks run that stretched the lead from 13 to 28. Gary Trent Jr. scored five quick points for Milwaukee, but it was too little, too late. Dalton Knecht, Pacôme Dadiet and Jose Alvarado got some run as the bench crew swept up and turned off the lights while the starters thought about their dinner plans.

Mo is basting his recap in delicious juices. The Knicks are back at the Garden tomorrow for another clash with the Cavs. Rest up, Knickerbockers.

#2801. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2800. As Jaguars go all-in for Super Bowl, it's time to praise them – and bury them | Opinion

I come to bury James Gladstone but also to praise him. For now.

The Jacksonville Jaguars' second-year general manager indulged his hyper-aggressive tendencies on Nov. 9, acquiring safety Jessie Bates III and defensive tackles Jordan Davis and Davon Godchaux in an obvious bid to further fuel his team's championship aspirations. And why not? The Jags are one of four NFL franchises to never appear in a Super Bowl – and if they fail to play on Valentine's Day this season, it won't be for lack of trying.

"We don't give enough credit or enough discussion about the Jags," former NFL head coach and current NFL Network analyst Steve Mariucci said Monday on "NFL GameDay Final." "Rodney Dangerfield, get no respect. Nobody talks about those guys.

"What did they do? They said, 'This is a window for us to win the darned Super Bowl. Let's load up our defense, because in the playoffs we're gonna be playing against some great quarterbacks, some MVP, great Hall of Fame-type quarterbacks.'"

NATE DAVIS: Vikings' Kirk Cousins deal might doom NFC West: NFL trade deadline winners, losers

Likely true. The Jaguars, who went 13-4 while winning the AFC South last year, couldn't escape their wild-card round matchup in Jacksonville against Josh Allen and the Buffalo Bills, who are currently locked in a race with the Jags in a bid for this year's No. 1 playoff seed in the AFC.

And yet as focused as Gladstone, 36, is on reaching for the brass ring, he's also drawn a clear line between himself and Philadelphia Eagles personnel czar Howie Roseman, with whom he brokered his biggest deal Monday. Moving forward, Jacksonville's title opportunity is quite likely akin to a fleeting aperture, while Roseman's Lombardi bay window could look out on the league for another decade or so … after this year's remodeling is done of course.

Should we fault Gladstone for trying to shed glory on a franchise that's rarely won, has long had one foot in Europe and – next year – will have both feet in Orlando while EverBank Stadium's $1.4 billion renovation is presumably completed? Not at all, even if only 42,000 fans can currently occupy the venue for what Gladstone and Co. hope are five more home dates this season, which presumes the Jags secure that top seed and win their playoff opener. After 31 mostly fruitless seasons, is this the time to strike with Trevor Lawrence belatedly justifying expectations that he could become a generational quarterback under the stewardship of second-year head coach Liam Coen? Yeah, probably.

NFL POWER RANKINGS: How does trade deadline affect pecking order, Jags' standing?

But as far as what the people of Duval County should expect in 2028, when their stadium reopens in hopes of filling its 65,000 or so seats?

▶ They should expect that CB/WR Travis Hunter is an impact player and performing at a level commensurate with the contract extension he'd be positioning himself for by then. Whether that actually happens is anyone's guess. Right now, the guy who was a two-way star for the University of Colorado and won the 2024 Heisman Trophy has decidedly not lived up to expectations after Gladstone surrendered a package to the Cleveland Browns that included two first-round picks and one in Round 2 in order to get Hunter with the No. 2 pick of the 2025 draft. Hunter has been better in Year 2 after an injury-curtailed rookie season, but that's hardly an achievement. He's not Darrelle Revis or Jalen Ramsey yet − not even close − but is starting for a foreboding defense. Offensively, he has seven catches this year. Gladstone needs him to truly flourish to justify the buy-in.

▶ They shouldn't expect to still have coordinators Grant Udinski (offense) and Anthony Campanile (defense), who both interviewed for head jobs earlier this year, and will almost certainly land ones in the next year or two. How effectively Gladstone and Coen, who's emerging into a superstar in his own right, are able to reload the staff remains an unknown that's likely to be tested in short order.

▶ They should expect WR Parker Washington to remain their primary offensive weapon … after Gladstone is obligated to sign him to a massive extension next year after Washington – wisely – turned down a less lucrative offer a few months ago. And don't forget, Gladstone acquired WR Jakobi Meyers at last year's trade deadline for fourth- and sixth-round picks before signing him to a three-year, $60 million extension. The team's approach to the receiver position is all the more befuddling given the investment in Hunter and Jacksonville's apparent unwillingness to trade Brian Thomas Jr., who's regressed from blossoming star as a rookie in 2024 to almost completely faded from the game plan nowadays.

▶ They should not expect this team to have a deeply set foundation. The Hunter trade has already limited the Jags' ability to import quantity from the past two drafts, though getting RB Bhayshul Tuten in the fourth round last year was a heist. But Gladstone has no first- or second-round pick next year, Bates costing him the latter selection at extreme cost given the 29-year-old will be a free agent next year, same as Godchaux.

▶ They should not expect Davis to be the second coming of John Henderson or Marcus Stroud. Yet Roseman extracted a 2027 first-rounder and 2029 second-rounder from Gladstone for Davis, a good player, though hardly a dominant one and with nary a Pro Bowl nod on his résumé.

▶ Barring a Super Bowl MVP or some such, they should not expect the Bates trade to age well. Make no mistake, he's a cagey player and first-rate leader. But giving up Round 2 (2027) and Round 5 (2028) selections for what might be a rental of a dozen games is unlikely to look wise in hindsight (if not foresight). Perhaps worse, the Buffalo Bills basically paid the same price Tuesday for another high-end safety, Amani Hooker (formerly of the Tennessee Titans) ... who also happens to be under contract through the 2028 season. Woof.

Jacksonville's approach seems almost diametrically opposed to that of Roseman, who tends to stockpile draft picks, nails a good percentage of the selections he makes and locks up his home-grown players with early extensions, all while maintaining the flexibility to target players like A.J. Brown in a trade or Saquon Barkley in free agency without seemingly ever crippling the roster.

And, yes, the Eagles' 2026 season has gone south for a variety of reasons. But Roseman is now in the process of laying the groundwork for the next iteration of the team. After spinning off Brown, Davis and offensive lineman Cam Jurgens over the past five months, Roseman has 10 total picks in the first two rounds of the next three NFL drafts. The ability to maximize and replenish his assets is why Roseman has positioned the Eagles, who have reached the Super Bowl three times in the past nine seasons and won it twice, alongside John Schneider's Seattle Seahawks as the NFC's most dominant organizations of the 21st century.

Based on the course they've charted, it's hard to envision the Jaguars achieving such consistent longevity. Gladstone, who worked in the Los Angeles Rams' front office for nine years before coming to Jacksonville and was part of the 2021 Super Bowl winners, has taken former boss Les Snead's "F--- them picks" to a new level. While the Jaguars are almost certainly better now than they were 10 days ago – when they lost starting safety Eric Murray and highly regarded defensive tackle DaVon Hamilton to season-ending injuries – Gladstone is also paying (overpaying?) for players without the pedigree to have nearly the same impact of stars like Ramsey, Von Miller or, certainly, Matthew Stafford, major difference-makers Snead once successfully imported at adjacent price points.

It's a major – and quite possibly myopic – gamble for an organization that's won one playoff game since the start of the 2018 season. And potentially a very costly one that will reverberate well into the future if Gladstone and the Jags have miscalculated.

All the NFL news on and off the field. Sign up for USA TODAY's 4th and Monday newsletter.

#2799. New York soccer has a problem: With NYCFC and Red Bulls missing the playoffs, can a new stadium and superstar signings save MLS's biggest market?

With neither MLS club making the playoffs and USL teams struggling to make an impact, the city needs a shot in the arm to revitalize its soccer scene

Hundreds gathered to celebrate a major construction milestone at Etihad Park, NYCFC's long-awaited soccer-specific stadium. There were workers, union members, and club staff. There were press, fans, and executives. The occasion was a topping-off ceremony, an age-old tradition in which a tree is placed on a steel beam and then, thanks to the awesome power of a rattling crane, hoisted to the top of what will soon become NYCFC's new home. And thus the final beam was in place, marking what was, in effect, the start of a new era of New York City soccer.

And in truth, this city needs it. For the first time since NYCFC entered MLS in 2015, neither they nor the New York Red Bulls will feature in the playoffs. That adds another layer to the league's struggle to capture its biggest market. A new stadium offers something to look forward to. This season's results have given New York considerably less to celebrate.

There have been moments to celebrate, though. NYCFC won MLS Cup in 2021 and reached the Eastern Conference final last year. The Red Bulls reached MLS Cup in 2024. The challenge is turning those achievements into the kind of lasting connection that keeps a club central to the city's sporting life.

New York has a complicated relationship with the beautiful game. Nowhere in North America is fandom so concentrated, and cultures so interwoven. There are millions of people here, from all over the place, who really like this sport. They support Manchester United, Liverpool or Real Madrid. They support Arsenal, Chelsea or Barcelona. There is literally a PSG store on Fifth Avenue.

But for years, the local clubs have struggled to turn that passion into something of their own.

The problem runs deeper than two disappointing seasons. So often, soccer conflates success with winning. You have to hoist a trophy to be relevant. And that, in some ways, is true. Yet more important than silverware is the vibe, the journey, the bit for fans to get behind. In an ideal world, the answer to 'why do you support your club?' should be so much more than 'they win stuff.'

And that, in 2026, is New York's problem. This soccer scene is so vibrant. Its mayor, Zohran Mamdani, is so, so passionate about the sport. But the local professional men's sides - from USL to MLS - are too often defined by their dysfunction. New York is primetime, spotlight, glitz and glamor. Soccer, everywhere else in the world, manages to embody those ideas. Yet in MLS's biggest market, its clubs are still fighting to become an essential part of the show. Another postseason without either of them only makes that harder. And that simply has to change.

NYCFC have been hurt by their inability to secure their own home stadium until recently.

This isn't necessarily any massive fault of the various clubs in the area. The problems are multiple here. The first is that of competition.

The issue is, in New York, everyone competes with everything. It is the biggest TV media market in America, and quite comfortably the entertainment capital of the world. There is no shortage of things to do here, and New Yorkers have no problem showing up for them. But when you're competing with the Knicks, Yankees, Broadway and seemingly endless other entertainment options, convincing people to embrace something new is a different challenge altogether. Getting them to go to soccer in America is difficult enough. Getting them to go to soccer in New York, where NYCFC rank 12th and the Red Bulls 20th in attendance according to worldfootball.net, is another proposition entirely.

And in America, soccer is very much an entertainment product on the periphery of things. Sure, there are millions of soccer fans here. And there are plenty of season ticket holders for both NYCFC and New York Red Bulls (NYCFC were delighted to announce they passed 20,000 'founding members' in 2015). But soccer is not on the same level as the Yankees or the Knicks - appointment viewing for aficionados and tourists alike. Soccer competes with a bar in Williamsburg, or a Broadway show. It competes with shopping in Soho, or a stroll in Central Park.

Try figuring out how to take thousands to blow off the prospects of all of that and make their way to a soccer game.

Take NYCFC, for example. This year, they split their regular-season home schedule between 10 games at Yankee Stadium and seven at Citi Field, fitting around the baseball calendar. Their permanent home is still under construction. They have recognizable players, including USMNT goalkeeper Matt Freese, but lack a global marquee name on the scale of Pirlo or Villa. In a city with endless ways to spend an afternoon, how do they make watching NYCFC something more New Yorkers build their weekends around?

Pirlo and Villa filled seats during their first season in New York - but success in MLS was elusive

Well, they have already toyed with one answer.

Andrea Pirlo was one of the biggest names in world football when he signed for NYCFC in 2015. Just a month before, he had played all 90 minutes of the 2015 Champions League Final as his beloved Juventus were beaten by Barcelona. Here Pirlo was, the old maestro. He was, in theory, the perfect star for NYCFC. The casuals might have heard the name - or watched him at a handful of international tournaments. The more vociferous fans knew him for his glittering career in Italian football, and recognized him as one of the best midfielders to ever play the game.

Add the fact that he was joining a side that already contained Frank Lampard and David Villa. Lampard took to Facebook - yes, there's an old sentiment - to welcome him. Pirlo looked so effortlessly cool in New York, roaming the streets in relative anonymity, all long hair and Italian shades. Two years before, Pirlo had released his autobiography, quite wonderfully named "I Think Therefore I Play."

And even if the football didn't work out immediately - NYCFC were mightily imbalanced and failed to make the playoffs in their first season - the stars helped sell tickets. The club announced that it had sold more than 1,000 additional founding memberships in less than a month following the July arrivals of Lampard and Pirlo. NYCFC also opened additional seating for several home games as demand grew. This thing felt big - even if the football didn't quite match.

Henry impressed for the Red Bulls, but his presence alone was not enough to fill Sports Illustrated Stadium

Five years earlier, New York Red Bulls had tried much the same thing with Thierry Henry. He suited New York, too, if in a slightly different way. The context of his arrival was different. Soccer in America was different back then. Yes, the USMNT had turned in an agreeable showing at the 2010 World Cup - topping their group but losing their first knockout game. But soccer, still, was not a hot commodity.

And so arrived Henry, fresh from three years with Barcelona, and widely regarded as the Premier League's GOAT. A club website reporter for the Red Bulls described the hype quite wonderfully:

"And David Beckham? You're no Thierry Henry."

He took a limo to his introductory press conference at Red Bull Arena. For his first game, he arrived by train from New York to New Jersey, just like everyone else.

But that fixture - quite poetically, a friendly against Tottenham - didn't sell out. That ran counter to the wishes of then-managing director Erik Stover, a grizzled vet of the New York soccer scene.

"We were very vocal on the board level; we felt it was very important for this league and for this team, and to fill [Red Bull Arena] on a regular basis, to have more than one or two designated players," he said in 2010.

For all the excitement around Henry's arrival - there were suggestions that he could come to MLS as early as 2008, such was his love for New York - the Red Bulls still struggled to turn that star power into a broader foothold in the city. And this time, the superstar's performances were hardly the problem.

He mentored the younger guys. He helped RBNY win the Supporters' Shield in 2013. He surpassed the 50-goal mark and left the Red Bulls as the club's all-time leader in assists. The connection lasted, too. When the Red Bulls and Barcelona played a friendly in 2022, Henry walked onto the pitch wearing a jersey split between the colors of his two former clubs.

Here was a superstar who had delivered. There were goals, silverware and a lasting affection for the team he had joined. Yet turning that into a place in New York's everyday sporting conversation remained a much bigger job. Henry had given people a reason to watch the Red Bulls. Making the club something they could not miss would take more.

Brunson became one of the biggest names in basketball after moving to the Knicks - and secured his legacy by winning an NBA Championship.

So, what's the point in those two chronicles? Well, they show both ends of the spectrum. One was a star that led to relative success - and drove interest in the game. Another is a star that filled seats - at least, in the early days - and yielded only massive underperformance.

But either way, both situations have been immortalized and filled with revisionism. Henry temporarily got a street named after him in New York (helped, you'd think, by the Arsenal fandom held by the city's pro-soccer mayor). Pirlo's time in New York has been immortalized. David Villa's, though tainted by off-field allegations, remains strong.

New York offers an enormous stage, but players still have to earn their place on it. Jalen Brunson's performances for the Knicks have driven his rise; the city's attention has magnified it. The Yankees' history brings a similar spotlight, along with the expectation that their biggest names will deliver. There is an opportunity for soccer here, too. A marquee signing could help NYCFC or the Red Bulls reach beyond their existing supporters. But as Henry showed, even getting that signing right is only part of the job. The larger challenge is giving people a reason to care about the club long after the star has gone.

Michael Bradley's Red Bulls played some exciting soccer, but failed to make the MLS playoffs

This is all the more important after a disappointing season for both MLS clubs. NYCFC and the Red Bulls failed to make the playoffs, leaving plenty to assess. NYCFC can point to injuries. The Red Bulls have promising young players, but the team could not turn that promise into a postseason place. When the MLS playoffs begin on November 19, both will be watching from home.

The region's newer men's teams have had a difficult year, too. There was genuine excitement when the Cosmos brand was relaunched in USL League One. But they finished second to last, with seven league wins. Backheeled's attendance tracker lists an average of 1,453 across 18 reported home matches at Hinchliffe Stadium in Paterson, New Jersey. Brooklyn FC, meanwhile, managed five league wins in their inaugural USL Championship campaign.

There is a different story in the women's game. Gotham FC won the NWSL Shield and remain in contention for consecutive championships. Their average attendance rose to 10,943, helped substantially by their Citi Field showcase, and finished slightly below the league's 11,003 average.

Yet even that success has come with complications. Concerns over the playing surface at Sports Illustrated Stadium forced Gotham to relocate their semifinal to the 5,000-seat Icahn Stadium. A team that earned the right to host one of its biggest games will now have room for considerably fewer supporters.

NYCFC parted ways with Pascal Jansen the day after their season ended, a harsh outcome for a coach who had taken them to the Eastern Conference final a year earlier. Now they must appoint a successor and decide how to strengthen the team ahead of the 2027 sprint season and the opening of Etihad Park.

The Red Bulls could create space for another marquee signing if Emil Forsberg or Eric Maxim Choupo-Moting departs. The timing remains important: Forsberg has reportedly agreed to stay through the sprint season before returning to Sweden, according to amNewYork, although the club has not confirmed that account. They also already have a young player worth watching in Julian Hall, a teenage USMNT forward from the Big Apple.

Investment simply has to be smart, though. Christian Pulisic, in whom NYCFC have publicly expressed interest, extended his Milan contract through 2031 on October 30, making a move harder to envisage in the near term. Gio Reyna is playing for Strasbourg in Ligue 1. Weston McKennie is under contract at Juventus until 2030.

Perhaps an established international star could help connect a club with more of the city's soccer communities. Sadio Mane, for example, could hold particular appeal for New York's Senegalese community. But any signing would still have to fit the team, perform and give people a reason to keep coming back.

And then there's the stadium. The renderings of Etihad Park look quite wonderful. It's glitzy, glamorous and forward-thinking. The stadium itself has, more or less, taken shape, too. It now feels real. Now, New York City soccer just needs to provide a product to match its home.

#2798. Knicks thrash woeful Bucks en route to fourth consecutive win

They stepped on the throats of an overmatched opponent early Tuesday night and didn't let go of the squeeze, pummeling the Bucks 117-85 at MSG.

It represented a fourth straight win for the Knicks (6-4), whose 0-3 stumble to start their title defense is firmly in the rearview.

Karl-Anthony Towns, who scored 21 points and grabbed 16 rebounds, goes up for a shot during the Knicks' 117-85 blowout win over the Bucks on Nov. 10, 2026 at the Garden. Charles Wenzelberg / New York Post

Jalen Brunson led the scoring with 23 points on 18 shots and Karl-Anthony Towns grabbed 16 boards with 21 points. It was Towns' 10th straight double-double to start the season, one off the franchise record set by Willis Reed in 1970.

"He continues to be a double-double machine at a high level," coach Mike Brown.

The Knicks benefited from a balanced attack, with OG Anunoby adding 19 points in 29 minutes.

But again, the Knicks rode their stout defense. They held the sad-sack Bucks (1-10) to 38 percent shooting. Their past five opponents have all scored fewer than 100 points, a rare streak in the 3-point era.

Jalen Brunson, who scored a game-high 23 points, goes up for a layup as Kyle Kuzma defends during the Knicks' win over the Bucks. Charles Wenzelberg / New York Post

"I just think that we're doing a great job of getting back to the basics. We started off slow, and now we're picking it up," said Towns, who was down on himself after the game for shooting 2-for-9 on treys. "While the shooting isn't doing great —

I could be the first one to tell you about that tonight — but I think we're doing a great job of executing right now at a high level, and our defense is there. At the end of the day, the offense will catch up. The defense is something you got to establish. We're doing a great job of that right now."

The game felt over by halftime, when the Knicks were up by 24 and the Bucks had only 34 points. The Knicks led the rebounding battle 34-17 at the break and ended the night with a staggering 70 boards. Milwaukee cut the deficit to 13 in the third quarter but never got it into single digits in the second half. The Knicks stomped Milwaukee 32-13 in the fourth quarter.

OG Anunoby, who scored 19 points, shoots a jumper during the Knicks' win over the Bucks. Charles Wenzelberg / New York Post

"Our group, they were pretty efficient tonight," Brown said. "I thought our physicality on the defensive end of the floor was at a pretty high level, and they did it the right way. They did it without fouling. Which was a lot of fun to watch. The point-of-attack defense, with Landry [Shamet] and especially Deuce [McBride] on the ball, was unbelievable. Deuce was our defensive player of the game. Could have gone to Landry as well. But just a fantastic job at the point of the screen in pick-and-rolls. [The Bucks] had two 13-point quarters and I'm sure they missed some shots, but I thought we also impacted their shots with our play."

The Knicks were without Josh Hart for a third consecutive game. He was out with an illness after missing two games because of ankle soreness. Shamet again started in Hart's spot and scored 10 points in 28 minutes.

New York also got solid bench contributions. Tyler Kolek continued to play over Jose Alvarado and finished with 12 points, four assists and a block. During Kolek's 15 minutes, the Knicks outscored the Bucks by 23. They were actually better with McBride on the floor. The backup guard was a plus-39 in 25 minutes.

New York Knicks center Andre Drummond passes to guard Jalen Brunson during the second quarter. Charles Wenzelberg / New York Post

Andre Drummond grabbed 14 rebounds in 16 minutes.

The Bucks are a disaster this season after trading Giannis Antetokounmpo to the Miami Heat. It didn't help Tuesday that their leading scorer — Tyler Herro — missed the game because of a knee contusion. Myles Turner, who is in the middle of a $109 million contract, was a lazy disaster with three points on 1-for-7 shooting while getting roasted on defense by Towns.

#2797. The Kirk Cousins contract points to a revised deal or termination after 2026

The last time the Vikings brought back a key former player for a third-round pick, the reunion didn’t last long. This time, it will last longer — but possibly not beyond the current season.

In 2010, the Vikings sent a third-round pick to the Patriots for receiver Randy Moss, whom the Vikings had traded to the Raiders in 2005. Former coach Brad Childress, calling Moss a “programmatic non-fit,” released Moss after four games.

Now, quarterback Kirk Cousins returns for a third-round pick, and a contract that covers nine regular-season games. His deal with the Raiders was structured to maximize the amount the Falcons owed Cousins for 2026 ($8.7 million, on top of the $90 million he made in 2024 and 2025) with another $10 million paid by the Raiders to Cousins in 2027, in the form of a guaranteed roster bonus not subject to offset.

The contract also includes a 2028 base salary of $78.655 million that becomes fully guaranteed on the fifth day of the 2027 league year. Coupled with a $1.345 million base salary in 2027 that becomes fully guaranteed that same day, the Vikings would be on the hook for $80 million over two years if he’s not released by then.

Without a revised deal, the current contract surely would be torn up by March. The question is whether the Vikings and Cousins would be able to come up with a new contract to replace it.

Much of that depends on how the rest of the year goes. Still, Cousins has the ability to force his way to free agency, for the fourth time in his career, by simply declining to re-do the existing deal.

However it plays out, Cousins can be counted on to make a good business decision. He has $333 million in career earnings, which has him at fourth on the all-time list.

On-field success is a different story. He has made it to the postseason three times in his career, with one playoff win.

If, as expected, the return of Cousins provides a B-12 shot to the Minnesota franchise, he has a realistic chance of getting back to the playoffs. And of winning a second postseason game.

And maybe a third. Hell, maybe even a fourth.

#2796. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2794. Terps in the NFL Week 9: Nick Cross has monster game in loss to Rams

Washington Commanders safety Nick Cross had a strong game despite a 20-13 loss against the Los Angeles Rams.

Cross made eight tackles, defended three passes, recovered a fumble and had a third-quarter interception against Rams quarterback Matthew Stafford. Sunday marked the 25-year-old's first created turnover of a season riddled by injuries thus far.

The Bowie, Maryland, native has just played in five games, spending time sidelined due to an undisclosed internal injury and illness. He missed roughly a month this season, absent from the field from Sept. 20 until Oct. 19.

Since returning, he has 19 tackles and has cemented himself as a key piece in Washington's secondary.

#2793. Celtics’ red-hot shooting, PG’s big night overshadow Brown-Tatum matchup in Sixers’ loss

The high-profile, max contract wing dropped 36 points to lead his new team to a win over his old team, just like everyone expected.

The Sixers were demolished 123-101 by the Boston Celtics thanks to a Paul George masterclass Tuesday night.

In his first game back against his old team, Jaylen Brown didn't have the night he envisioned, putting up just 13 shooting 4-of-9 from the field, sitting for much of the second half in foul trouble.

Tyrese Maxey was the entire Sixers' offense for too much of the night, leading the team with 24 on 8-of-12 shooting with four assists. Joel Embiid was able to get to the line but still struggled from the floor, finishing with 16 points, six rebounds and one assist shooting 2-of-9 from the floor.

LeBron James struggled as well, having 11 points on 4-of-12 shooting along with eight rebounds and five assists. George led all scorers with his 36.

The Sixers were once again without Adem Bona (neck) and Dominick Barlow (back).

Revenge was had on the first bucket of the game as Paul George was able to drive through Maxey for a midrange pull-up, just like everyone anticipated. He did the same over VJ Edgecombe a couple possessions later, but did foul Embiid to help the Sixers' score their first points of the game.

Brown's first shot against his old team was an open corner three swung to him that didn't fall. When he got Jayson Tatum matched up on him, the crowd came to a roar, but Tatum won the first round as he was able to force a turnover with the help of a double team. As a team the Sixers had some sloppiness to start the game. Embiid had an ugly turnover trying to throw a behind-the-back pass, and they completely lost Tatum on a backdoor cut for an open layup.

Maxey got off to a tremendous start, one that the Sixers very much needed as the rest of the starters opened the game 0-of-5 from the floor. His shotmaking from all over the floor prevented the Sixers from going down into a big hole early. Desperate to make something happen, Brown did a good job to steal an outlet pass, but Derrick White was able to slide under and take a charge to discount the basket. He finally made his first field goal of the game driving by Jordan Walsh to slam it home.

While he didn't hit a jumper to break the seal, the Sixers offense came alive more with Embiid getting to the line. It's clear he's still trying to find that trust in his jumper again, but the way he was getting fouled in the post made him look a bit more like himself. VJ Edgecombe made a brilliant spin move to cut the lead to three.

That bucket was followed by Embiid's second turnover of the game, a contested Maxey fadeaway, and Embiid missing two out of three from the free throw line. Maxey tried to hit Dean Wade in the dunker finishing a drive, but Wade wasn't ready for it and dropped it out of bounds. Anfernee Simons hit a pull-up jumper to end a field goal drought that lasted over four and a half minutes and cut the Sixers' deficit to seven points after the first.

The end of the first and beginning of the second made clear the Sixers were leaning more into staggering Brown and James together, leaving the three incumbent starters together. LeBron was also scoreless in the first and hit a midrange early in the second to get on the board. He also found Simons in the corner from three, but with the Celtics scoring on four of their first five possessions of the quarter the Sixers continued to fall behind.

Nothing emphasized the Sixers' struggles to get anything to fall like their struggles around the basket. LeBron smoked his second bunny of the game, then tried to hit Jabari Walker right by the basket, but Walker couldn't corral it and lost the ball out of bounds. They started the game shooting 44% at the rim, which Cleaning the Glass put in just the third percentile across the league.

After a rare made basket, the Sixers were too slow getting back and conceded an open three to Sam Hauser before anyone had gotten back. Brown missing a pair of free throws followed by Hauser hitting a very contested three off the dribble made this game feel like it was starting to spiral. Coming out of a timeout, George beat Brown for an offensive rebound and drew a foul as he banked in his putback.

The combination of this game's struggles at the rim and the season's struggles in transition all came to a head on one play. LeBron hit Embiid with a touchdown pass and Embiid took off a little too early and missed the layup, and got his feet tangled up with George as they landed. Edgecombe was able to find the loose ball and slam it back home, but Embiid stayed on the ground for a few moments and hobbled his way through the final 30 seconds of the half. The Sixers trailed by 11 at the break.

Despite that scare, Embiid was not only out there to start the second half, but he started it by making his first three-point attempt since the home opener. Maxey was able to get to the line on the Sixers next possession, but the Celtics halted any sort of momentum right there. Tatum hit a contested three over Maxey before Brown picked up his fourth foul of the night.

LeBron was also desperate for a jumper to fall and it took him two attempts in the third to finally get a three to go down. Edgecombe, who was easily one of the best Sixers on the floor so far, followed that up with a very acrobatic finish over Neemias Queta. Maxey was able to get to the basket on the next possession, cutting the lead to six and prompting a Celtics timeout.

The Sixers continued to mount momentum coming out of the timeout. Embiid was still able to get to the line and LeBron started getting downhill as well. They did a good job of not letting big Celtics baskets kill their momentum. Tatum got LeBron pretty good with a dunk and George buried another three, but the Sixers kept eating into the lead.

Wade was putting in a great night for the Sixers, and it helped that he got some threes up and buried a couple, one of which briefly tied the game up. Maxey hit a ridiculous three to keep it tied for a second, but Boston had another onslaught of their own to go back in front. One hit off the top of the backboard, but it also didn't help that the Sixers kept leaving Hauser and Tatum wide open on the perimeter. Wade had an open three rim out to keep the Celtics up by nine.

The Celtics drilled three more triples before the Sixers got on the board to start the quarter, prompting a timeout from Nick Nurse. The full starting five checked back into the game much earlier than normal in one last attempt to salvage the night. The first basket coming out of that timeout was George posting up Maxey, hitting his pull-up while getting fouled in the process.

George scooted past the entire Sixers defense for a layup on the following possession. Maxey appeared to have earned a trip to the line for the Sixers first points of the quarter, but Joe Mazzulla challenged the play and got it overturned. The Sixers didn't score their first points of the fourth until the 7:37 mark when a Brown three bounced in. The Sixers waived the white flag after a couple more minutes of George burying threes on them.

#2792. Request for review of Celtic Park security operations vetoed by Board

The North stand viewed from the North Curve. Celtic 3 Hearts 1. 16 May 2026. Photo Vagelis Georgariou

While this seems a perfectly fair and reasonable request, if supporters have legitimate concerns on this hugely important matter, the resolution (17) at AGM on Tuesday 1st December will be rejected as the Board are advising shareholders to vote it down. The block vote of Dermot Desmond and his cohorts will ensure that the matter will go no further than any brief discussion that is allowed.

Celtic supporters at Paradise last night. 3rd August 2026; Celtic Park, Scottish Premiership, Celtic versus Dundee; Celtic fans David Young IMAGO/ Action Plus

Here's the Celtic Board's advice to shareholders ahead of the AGM as they advise shareholders to vote against the resolution.

'The Board takes the safety, security and wellbeing of supporters very seriously and welcomes the opportunity to set out its position clearly. The safety and security of every person who attends CelticPark, and who travels to follow Celtic away from home, is the Company's highest operational priority.

'The Company works with other stakeholders as well as external consultants on a regular basis to review and continually improve its safety and security arrangements. The Company's Safety and Security team is effectively led, very strong and vastly experienced, and the Board does not accept the characterisation of the department in the statement provided in support of the shareholders' requisition.

Celtic v theRangers – Scottish Premiership – Celtic Supporters hold up a Let Celtic Flourish banner during the match at Celtic Park on Sunday September 1, 2024. Photo Steve Welsh

'It should be noted that there have been instances of unacceptable conduct within Celtic Park that the Club is required to address. The Board wishes to reiterate that the measures introduced in and around the rail seating section were implemented following assessments of crowd flow and safety risk, in accordance with the Company's obligations under its safety certificate, in line with its commitment to supporter safety and in dialogue with the appropriate authorities.

'While some supporters may be frustrated by the introduction of these measures, the Board notes that they are both necessary and proportionate for ensuring supporter safety.

'The Board is also aware of the concerns that supporters have raised relating to incidents and crowd management at away fixtures. The Company does not control, and cannot direct, the safety and security at third-party stadiums but is always keen to advocate for supporters when issues are raised.

8th March 2026 Ibrox , Scottish Cup, Rangers versus Celtic. The Celtic Board in the main stand. Photo Vagelis Georgariou

'Where Celtic supporters have raised concerns about their experience at away fixtures, the Company has taken on this feedback through its Supporter Liaison Officer ("SLO") and, where appropriate, has raised supporter concerns with the appropriate parties. The Board will ensure that the Company and its SLO function continues to listen to feedback from supporters in respect of their experience at away fixtures and robustly advocate for supporters where change is necessary.'

#2791. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Democrats' midterm election win has put the artificial intelligence trade in question, as voters clearly expressed opposition to the rapid buildout of data centers. The stock market could remain volatile amid AI trade concerns, macro uncertainties, and geopolitical tensions.

In this scenario, investors can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios to earn steady income. However, the extensive universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts assign ratings after in-depth financial analysis.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Viper Energy (VNOM) is a subsidiary of Diamondback and owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests, primarily in the Permian Basin. The company reported upbeat third-quarter earnings, supported by recent acquisitions and continued development activity from Diamondback and third-party operators. Viper announced a base cash dividend of 50 cents per share for Q3 2026, payable on Nov. 19. Based on the base and variable dividends announced over the past 12 months, VNOM's dividend yield stands at 5.7%.

Following the Q3 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper Energy stock with a price target of $61. The analyst noted that the company delivered solid Q3 results, with production, including oil and natural gas liquids, exceeding both TD Cowen and consensus estimates.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the key update was the announcement of two royalty acquisitions for $809 million in cash, including deferred consideration, and $82 million of newly issued shares.

Given the recently announced base dividend hike, Bilkoski expects Viper to direct incremental free cash flow toward buybacks. While the acquisitions increase leverage, the analyst believes that Viper has enough capacity for opportunistic buybacks.

Bilkoski noted that Viper has historically delivered one of the highest production per share growth profiles in his royalty coverage, but still trades at a notable discount to the royalty peer average and the company's historical FCF yield range. "In our view, its above-average growth warrants a higher valuation," said the analyst.

Bilkoski ranks No. 525 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.80%. See Viper Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Exxon Mobil (XOM) reported robust Q3 2026 profits, as its U.S. refineries boosted production to capitalize on the high diesel fuel prices sparked by the conflict in the Middle East. The oil and gas giant announced a quarterly dividend of $1.07 per share, payable on Dec. 10, reflecting a 3.9% increase. This marks Exxon's 44th consecutive year of dividend increases. At an annualized dividend of $4.28 per share, XOM offers a yield of 2.53%.

Impressed by the Q3 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Exxon stock and raised his price target to $193 from $180 to reflect higher downstream earnings, with the expectation of increased mid-cycle margins.

Gabelman noted that the strength in Exxon's refining business continues to offset Middle East-related headwinds. He added that the company increased its ownership in LNG projects, which should reduce its Middle East concentration over time from 75% to 50% and lower the likelihood of diversification-focused mergers and acquisitions. Beyond LNG, Gabelman expects Exxon's growth in the 2030s to come from Guyana, Kashagan, Permian, and possibly Venezuela.

Gabelman noted that Exxon announced a dividend hike of nearly 4% and plans to maintain buybacks at $20 billion for 2027. "The decision to maintain reduces the pro-cyclicality nature of the buyback," said the analyst.

Gabelman ranks No. 347 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.90%. See Exxon Mobil Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Williams (WMB), which owns and operates energy infrastructure to deliver natural gas, reported strong third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of $0.525 per share, or $2.10 per share annualized, payable on Dec. 28. WMB offers a dividend yield of 3.09%.

In reaction to the Q3 2026 results, CIBC analyst Robert Catellier reaffirmed a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $82. The analyst noted that Q3 results were essentially in line with expectations and reflected continued strength in execution across WMB's infrastructure portfolio.

Catellier said that Williams' Q3 performance was driven by higher transmission, storage, and power generation revenues, as well as contributions from the Momentum Midstream acquisition.

He noted that Williams maintained its full-year EBITDA guidance of $8.3 billion to $8.5 billion. In fact, results are tracking toward the higher end of the guidance range, backed by contributions from recently completed projects and the Momentum Midstream deal. WMB continues to target long-term EBITDA compound annual growth rate of at least 11% through 2030.

Catellier expects WMB's long-term targets to be supported by incremental transmission and storage opportunities, including Transco's Southeast Supply Enhancement II project and an 84 MMcf/d expansion of the Garden Connector project.

Catellier ranks No. 667 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.60%. See Williams Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2790. Vikings announce return of Kirk Cousins

The Vikings have announced the trade that brings Cousins from the Raiders for a third-round pick in the 2027 draft. The deal hinges on Cousins passing a physical, which is usually (but not always) a formality.

The Vikings would be out of luck at quarterback if Cousins fails the physical. That will make them far more likely to give it a passing grade.

Cousins spent 2018 through 2023 with the Vikings. His return undoubtedly means he’ll become the instant starter, with Carson Wentz undoubtedly being released.

There’s no reason to part ways with Kyler Murray. At $1.3 million, he’s a low-cost insurance policy, in the event of a Cousins injury. It remains to be seen whether Murray or Max Brosmer will be the primary backup to Cousins.

#2788. The glaring issue holding back Nets as team rallies together to turnaround slow start

DALLAS — When Benjamin Franklin said, "We must all hang together, or assuredly we shall all hang separately," the sentiment was about building a country.

The Nets are finding that out the hard way.

They enter Wednesday's tilt in New Orleans a disheartening 2-8. And with a rare road practice the day before in Dallas, the Nets went in intent on keeping the morale up, the energy positive and — most importantly — the team together.

Julius Randle drives on Jalen Johnson on during the Nets' loss to the Hawks on Nov. 7, 2026 in Atlanta. Getty Images

"That was one of my points after the [Dallas] game; it's what we need to not only get better, but also come together," Jordi Fernández said. "Our practice, the energy was outstanding and we got better. So right now we want to go out there and play a good brand of basketball."

The Nets had played a bad brand of ball in their 130-93 loss to the Mavericks, a desultory defeat in which they were disjointed and disconnected.

Getting on the same page was a main point of emphasis in their practice, as was getting in a good headspace.

"There's a lot of things to be down about. But it's a long season, and if we get down already, then it's going to be a longer season," admitted Michael Porter Jr. "So coming here with good energy and get better was what we needed to do."

The proof will be in the pudding, or against the Pelicans.

Brooklyn was disorganized offensively against the Mavericks, with 21 turnovers resulting in 25 points.

It's a recurring theme after coughing up 22 giveaways for 28 points the night before in a heartbreaking 124-121 loss in Atlanta.

"Yeah, it's pretty simple, right? I know the guys are not trying to turn it over, so put less pressure on yourself when you make those decisions," said Fernández. "It's more about our spacing and then how we do from there because we're pretty unorganized early and then that doesn't help. So I don't want them to play tentative; I want them to play aggressive, and making their decisions and playing very hard.

"And then defensively it's going back to what is going to make us be a very good defensive team is ball pressure first. Build our trust through communication, our coverages, and then finish with multiple efforts. We've shown it at times, and now in this next set of five games we want to see improvement."

The turnover issue has been death by a thousand cuts. And worst of all, those slices are self-inflicted wounds.

Shoddy spacing, bad reads and similar gaffes lead to giveaways.

Brooklyn began Tuesday third worst in the league in turnovers and turnover percentage, and fourth worst in points off those giveaways.

Michael Porter Jr. handles the ball during the Nets' blowout loss to the Mavericks on Nov. 8, 2026 in Dallas. Getty Images

"That's what we're going over, so that's all I know," said Day'Ron Sharpe. "Especially transition, just get into our spots of transition and just being aggressive and making the right play. We're all basketball players, we all know what to do, so just having faith in what we work on and hopefully that transitions to win."

Knowing what to do hasn't always translated on the fly.

"(It's) reading how the defense is guarding us. Sometimes they're switching and we're not having the right reads off of that," said Porter. "So we slowed down some parts of practice and walked through the different ways we can attack different coverages when teams are staying with their own vs. switching vs. top locking. All those things will help.

"I mean, we've got no choice. Any trial or tribulation can be viewed as a lesson, and that's what we're choosing to do. We're just trying to grow as a team because we're stuck with each other. And we're right where we wanna be: here. So we have no choice but to get better."

#2787. Panthers vs Broncos game review: The other stuff

This was the worst loss against the Denver Broncos since, well, you know. At least this one was different, I suppose. Could you imagine if the game on Sunday was surrounded in controversy due to some highly questionable refereeing that seemed to heavily favor one side and for all intents and purposes ended on a sack-fumble? That would have been traumatic.

The obvious route to take this game review would be to list out and dwell on the many aspects of the game that boiled my blood. I will even encourage you to do so yourselves in the comments and I'll join in, as there's probably something cathartic about listing out the laundry list of frustrations stemming from Sunday. That said, rather than add on to that vitriol here, I'm going to take a different direction for the sake of our collective sanity.

Let's take a moment to look back and acknowledge the encouraging moments from Sunday that would have received some praise and fanfare had there note been all the rest of the other nonsense taking up the conversation.

Young had had back-to-back games with less than encouraging performances. While far from being the central issue or reason for the disappointing performances given the state of the offensive line and the quality of football being played by the defense, the downward tick in performance coupled with rumors of a contract extension led to some negative, even if somewhat warranted, takes being spread on social media.

Despite Facing a defense that's near the top the league at creating pressure and producing takeaways, Young found a way to be productive and find ways to move the ball. 276 yards, 3 touchdowns on 71.8% completed passes would get the job done on most NFL Sundays.

After Sunday's 8 reception, 90 yard performance Darren Waller is up to 299 receiving yards this season. For comparison's sake, that already surpasses Tommy Trembles 249 yards in 2025 which lead the position in receiving yards. Speaking of Tremble, those 249 yards were his career high to date and he currently stands about 80 yards short of that total in in 2026.

There were a handful of splash plays intertwined with the baker's dozen of penalties and other mid-play meltdowns. Tetairoa McMillan continues to improve and produce when needed, including a critical first down as well as a touchdown while lined up against Patrick Surtain. Lee Hunter flashed on multiple plays including an outstanding tackle for loss early in the contest that will surely earn him more than the 13 snaps he played Sunday. Chau Smith-Wade had multiple touchdown-saving pass break-ups while Corey Thornton baited Bo Nix to throw him his first interception of his career.

The Panthers rush defense has some Dr. Jekyll and Mr Hyde thing going on, but overall rank as the 28th rush defense in the NFL. Holding the Broncos to 82 yards and 3 yards per carry is certainly an improvement for the newish-look defense that Todd Wash is trying to implement. Let's hope they can make a trend out of keeping the opponents to less than 100 yards rushing a game.

It wouldn't be an article on the Panthers if there isn't mention of the injuries currently plaguing the roster. Dave Canales spoke on Monday and mentioned that the practice window for Xavier Legette will be opened this week and that Jaycee Horn, Mike Jackson and Jonathon Brooks are close. Brady Christensen is likely to return in the next couple weeks as well to boast the offensive line one way or another. Fingers firmly crossed for the returns of Damien Lewis, Bobby Okereke, and Jaelan Philips who all remain on the active roster.

There's a lot to be mad about right now. But there's also a lot of positive aspects and improvements lying underneath.

We'll meet back here next week after the Panthers take on the New Orleans Saints for the first time this season. Until then, keep pounding.

#2784. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."

#2781. CFP Earthquake Sees Georgia Retake #1

Georgia now sits at the top of the College Football Playoff rankings after a wild Tuesday night release. The Bulldogs climbed to the No. 1 spot while Notre Dame jumped six places to land at No. 5. This new order leaves Miami out in the cold after they dropped six spots despite a loss by just one score. The selection committee made bold moves that left fans scratching their heads and teams scrambling to fix their resumes.

Georgia's blowout win over Mississippi on Nov. 7 cleared the path for the Bulldogs to claim the number one rank. The committee saw enough to push them ahead of Utah and Alabama in a tight field at the top. During a call with reporters, Hunter Yurachek, chair of the 13-person selection committee, addressed the shift. Miami's fall was severe for a team that lost on the road without their star player Malachi Toney. The Hurricanes slipped from the top spot to No. 7 while Notre Dame leaped over them thanks to a win over the Canes. Yurachek explained the logic behind the drop in a statement to the New York Post, "Then some things that happened around Miami in the rankings, we talked about that. Georgia had their big win at Ole Miss. Utah, their win over BYU. Alabama won at LSU, Texas at Missouri, UCLA at Minnesota. You had that combination of Miami losing as the No. 1 team, then some five teams behind them having some really signature wins. The committee thought that moving those teams ahead of Miami was the right thing to do in this ranking."

Indiana took a hard hit by dropping five spots to No. 9 even though they did not play last weekend. The Hoosiers sat out during a bye week while several teams they had beaten lost their own games. Yurachek blamed the timing of their rest for the slide. He noted that Indiana was "probably a victim of having a bye week at the wrong time." This situation hurt their strength of schedule as other teams improved while they waited.

UCLA rose four spots to No. 4 after a two-touchdown win over Minnesota on the road. The Bruins are now 9-0 and look like a top-four team in the eyes of the committee. Yurachek said the win made the Bruins "look like the number-four team in the country." Their defense has been steady all season while their offense found a new spark with backup quarterback Ty Dieffenbach. Dieffenbach threw 75-yard touchdown passes on back-to-back plays to seal the victory.

The committee noted that UCLA and Utah are the only two unbeaten teams left in major college football. Yurachek highlighted how rare this is this late in the season. "This is one of the earliest dates there's only been two undefeated teams remaining," Yurachek said. The rest of the top 12 includes Texas, Tennessee, BYU, Kentucky, and Texas Tech, with the Red Raiders set to be the first team left out.

Before the bracket is finalized on Dec. 6, three more Tuesday-night rankings shows will air.
